## THE PARTS VOLKSWAGEN DIDN'T MAKE — An exploded axonometric in which the bus stays whole and only the taqueria comes apart — every floating component on a leader line, and the leader lines are the bulb string.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `t12-1`

**THE IMAGE.** Portrait sheet. The VW body is drawn ONCE, whole, dead centre, at a true orthographic three-quarter (axonometric — parallel projection, no vanishing point), and it is a GHOST: 0.18 mm line only, no fill, in a 25 % tint of the warm near-black, so it reads as an absence, a diagram of a vehicle rather than a vehicle. It does not compete for a single second.

Everything Tacombi added floats off it in full, saturated, flat colour with a hard 0.4 mm keyline — a hierarchy inversion that lands instantly: the expensive thing is the ghost, the cheap hand-built things are the subject.

The exploded parts, each on its own vector, each one a real named object in the build: the CANTILEVERED COUNTER SLAB floating out and forward on the +Y vector with its brass nosing strip separated a further 40 mm below it and its tan laminate top 40 mm above — three plates in a stack, the way a fabrication drawing separates a laminate. The MURAL LID lifting straight up on the +Z vector, mural face rotated toward the viewer so the flowers and the olive-yellow menu strips read flat and full — this is the largest and loudest object on the sheet and it should be. The TAIL BOARD with its stay and its bulb row, floating aft on its own 38° axis. The THREE BOBBLE FRINGES as three separate scalloped arcs. The THREE MENU CARDS fanned like a hand of playing cards. The LOUVRE BLOCK exploded further into its TEN individual blades on a 0.88 mm pitch — a small dense burst of parallel strokes that gives the composition its one passage of fine texture. The '1963' PLATE FRAME, empty, floating alone with more white space around it than anything else on the sheet. The FOLK ART as a single translucent decal skin peeling off the flank like a transfer, still bus-shaped, hanging in the air.

AND THE JOKE THAT MAKES IT: the leader lines connecting every floating part back to its station on the ghost are not drafting lines. They are the BULB STRING — a continuous warm-amber beaded path, each callout leader made of little filled circles at a regular pitch, running from the part to its home. So the diagram's connective tissue and the vehicle's only self-luminous element are literally the same line. In the closed configuration that string traces the drip rail, the roof dome and the tail board's edge; here it has come apart and is doing the pointing.

Callouts hand-lettered, Spanish first, in the signwriter's voice, each with a factory-style part number that is a joke and a fact at once: `01 · MOSTRADOR — cantilevered 252 mm past the body` · `02 · TAPA PINTADA — width = f(angle)` · `03 · TABLA TRASERA — 38°, propped` · `04 · FLECOS — white bobble, ×3 apertures` · `05 · CARTAS — 96 × 311 mm` · `06 · REJILLA — 10 blades @ 21.1 mm` · `07 · 1963 — empty`.

Bottom of the sheet, one line, mono, small: `PARTS SUPPLIED BY THE FACTORY: 0`.

MOTION VERSION: same drawing, and every part flies IN and lands, hard-cut on a beat, 8–12 fps with holds — never a 24 fps tween. Last beat: the bulb string ignites nose to tail and the ghost fills with colour. Three seconds. The wheels never turn — this vehicle has no engine and no gearbox and must never be animated driving.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Delight, and then a small swell of pride on the brand's behalf. Exploded diagrams are a childhood pleasure — the Haynes manual, the LEGO instruction, the cutaway in the annual — and everyone who has ever loved one is disarmed by this format before they read a word of it. Then the inversion lands: you realise the famous vehicle is the faint grey thing and the SHELF, the FRINGE, the MENU CARDS are the ones drawn in colour. That is the whole company's story delivered without a sentence of copy — we did not buy an icon, we bought a wreck for three thousand dollars and added a restaurant to it. 'PARTS SUPPLIED BY THE FACTORY: 0' is the line that makes people laugh out loud and then buy it. And the bulb-string leader lines give the sheet warmth that no drafting document is supposed to have: the diagram is lit.

**FORMATS.** B2 portrait (500 × 707 mm) as the hero print — the format wants portrait because the lid lifts and the counter reaches, so the composition is a vertical burst.  ·  700 × 1000 mm for the restaurant wall, the same file at the same scale. The louvre-blade burst and the fringe arcs both survive: at that size the blades are 3.1 mm apart, which is where fine texture becomes pleasure rather than mush.  ·  1000 × 1000 mm square adaptation for a mural or a window vinyl, ghost bus reduced to silhouette only.  ·  Tote panel, 300 × 380 mm, four inks, folk-art decal skin dropped (it will fill in at that reduction) and the parts count reduced to five.  ·  3-second animation, 1:1 and 9:16, for social. Stepped, held, silent — and sound-designed even when muted: the lid should overshoot 2–3° and settle, which is the strut clacking home.  ·  A pull-out A3 'instruction sheet' insert for a merchandise box, printed one colour on newsprint, deliberately cheap.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** The exploded-view move is a genuine trap — it is the obvious way to dramatise a 3D model and it is a saturated stock category. It escapes on one structural decision, and the decision is the concept: A NORMAL EXPLODED VIEW DISASSEMBLES THE MACHINE; THIS ONE ONLY DISASSEMBLES THE PARTS THE FACTORY NEVER MADE. That single inversion means it cannot be produced from a stock VW asset at all — you cannot explode a counter, a mural lid and a bobble fringe off a bus that does not have them. It is also the strongest available answer to the trademark question, because every floating component is Tacombi's and the one thing that stays grey, faint and un-celebrated is Volkswagen's. And it deliberately omits the VW roundel from the exploded set, which is both correct (a pressing is not part of the conversion) and convenient (it is the owner's ninth reported defect and is still ~0.09 short of its own control ceiling).

**PRODUCTION.** The ghost body must NEVER be a render — it must be the vector line pass, tinted. A screened-back photoreal render behind flat colour art is the single most common way this composition goes ugly; it turns into a stock-photo collage. Line only.

The axonometric matters and is not negotiable: parallel projection, no perspective. A 3D exploded view in perspective reads as a product render; in axonometric it reads as a document. Standard 7°/42° or true isometric 30°/30° — pick one, declare it on the sheet, and note that no such camera exists in the tree today (studio.views()['topdown'] is a 62 mm PERSPECTIVE lens and has never been rendered by any preview list).

Ink order for screen print: ghost tint last, over everything, so the parts sit in front of it optically as well as compositionally.

HONESTY ON THE SHEET: the callout figures are a mix of measured and posed and the drawing should carry that in its own small way rather than pretending. `38°` is measured ±2.3. `10 blades @ 21.1 mm` is measured. `width = f(angle)` is literally true. But the louvre APERTURE is 7.0 mm and the source comment says 'INFERRED, not measured -- 1.5 px in', so do not dimension it. Where a figure is a pose, use the bracket convention from the Uncertainty Plate — the two posters should be visibly the same document family.

DO NOT explode the folk art as separate rosettes and tendrils. folk_gen.py's own census says the largest three connected components carry 84.4 % of the gold and 48 of 66 components carry 2.29 % between them — so the decal skin is drawn as three masses and the forty-eight are deleted. That is a measured licence to simplify, not a taste call, and it is what keeps the sheet from turning to noise at tote scale.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** A .BLEND-BASED PIPELINE, AND IT IS THE REAL COST. Every pose in this tree is BAKED INTO VERTICES — the hinge helpers rewrite v.co in place — so today one configuration equals one full rebuild and per-object transforms are impossible. build.py's only persistence is `if os.environ.get("T1_SAVE"): bpy.ops.wm.save_as_mainfile(...)`, and T1_SAVE appears in ZERO handoff documents (not the brief, not HANDOFF_CARRIERS.md, not CLAUDE.md, not README.md, not START_HERE.md, not OPEN_FINDINGS.md). Save one .blend, carry T1_SAVE by name into a carrier, and per-object explosion becomes keyframing instead of engineering.  ·  AN AXONOMETRIC CAMERA. Add one orthographic three-quarter view dict to studio.views(). Trivial to write, never done — ten of the fourteen defined cameras have not been exercised in at least ten revisions.  ·  A LINE PASS AND AN OBJECT-ID PASS. The ghost needs vector line; separating the conversion parts from the body needs object indices. `grep pass_index|use_pass` returns nothing in this tree outside the denoiser config — there is NO pass infrastructure at all. Build it modelled on matte_tap's colour contract (color_management='OVERRIDE', view_transform='Raw', look 'None', gamma 1.0), or every pass comes back S-curved through AgX. Drive pass_index off build.py's own ASSIGN list so the ID islands map to the 17 material keys for free. Watched kill: render the rig with the vehicle deleted and confirm object index is identically zero.  ·  THE FOLK-ART SKIN HAS NO MESH. This is the one part in the exploded set that is not an object. The flank art is a BOX-PROJECTED shader texture (t1_mats: swirl.png / swirl_b.png, projection='BOX'), so there is nothing to float. Either bake it to a decal plane conformed to the flank, or draw the skin in 2D from the 4096² master. Say which — an unnamed gap here is exactly the kind of thing that gets discovered in artwork rather than in planning.  ·  EVERY OTHER PART IS ALREADY A SEPARATE, NAMED OBJECT and needs no modelling: counter, counter_nosing, counter_top, tail_board, tail_board_stay, tb_bulbs, tb_bulbflex, tb_edge_red, tb_edge_dark, lid_board, lid_main, lid_rail ×2, lid_strut ×2, fringe ×3, menucard ×3, bulb_string, plate, plate_digits, louvres ×2, louvbay ×2, calidad_L, script_L, script_R.  ·  FOR THE MOTION VERSION ONLY — FIX THE LID COUPLING FIRST. LID_W = (LID_X0 − LID_X1) / LID_ASPECT / sin(LID_OPEN_DEG), so the mural board's width is tied to its angle: 1.2237 m at the shipped 76°, 3.4718 m at 20°, 6.8379 m at 10°, and a ZeroDivisionError at 0°. A naive T1_LIDDEG sweep does not close the lid, it inflates it to longer than the bus. Freeze LID_W at its 76° value behind a T1_LID_WCOUPLE ablation, add a guard row that the built panel's width does not move when the angle does, and watch that row fail on the coupled build first. The nearby `assert _top.co.y > LID_Y_HINGE + 0.010` also needs a closed-end branch, with the cause named in the row's own label.  ·  SEED post.py's GRAIN FROM THE FRAME NUMBER before any sequence is graded — it is fixed at seed=7, so identical grain lands on every frame and reads as dirt on the lens rather than film grain.

**SCREENER.** The bulb string AS the leader lines is the best single formal invention here — the diagram's connective tissue and the vehicle's only self-luminous element are the same line. The hierarchy inversion (the famous VW a 25% ghost, the fringe and menu cards in saturated flat colour) tells the whole company story with no copy, and 'PARTS SUPPLIED BY THE FACTORY: 0' is a line people buy. Honest that the folk art has no mesh to float (box-projected shader) and that no pass infrastructure exists at all. Exploded views are a saturated stock category; the inversion is what saves it.

---

## THE DEVELOPMENT — an impossible view you cut out and fold — The bus drawn as a sheet-metal development: flanks, roof, nose, tail and underside unfolded flat into one impossible orthographic plan, with fold lines, darts, tabs and a cut path — a real drafting form that is also a paper model a child assembles at the table.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `t12-2`

**THE IMAGE.** One large sheet, printed both sides on 300 gsm uncoated card. The recto is the DEVELOPMENT: the entire vehicle's skin unfolded flat into a single connected plan, laid out the way a fabricator draws a folded part before it goes to the brake press. The show flank lies across the middle at full length carrying its three serving apertures as ACTUAL DIE-CUT HOLES with the folk art and the silver wordmark printed around them. Hinged off its top edge on a dashed fold line, the roof lies above, and hinged off the roof, the off-side flank lies above that, upside down. Below the show flank, the underside — genuinely never seen by anyone, in any photograph, ever — printed with its chassis rails, underpan and the two closing panels. Nose and tail unfold off the ends. The counter slab, the mural lid, the tail board and the bulb string are separate small nets on the margins with their own tabs.

Every drafting convention is real and load-bearing: FOLD LINES as long-dash chain, CUT PATH as solid, TABS as small trapezoids labelled with the panel they glue to, DARTS as narrow triangles cut into the roof crown and the wheel arch mouths — because the body is doubly curved and cannot honestly develop flat, so the drawing puts the darts in and SAYS SO in a note. The wheel arches are true circular cutouts. The louvre block is printed as ten strokes, not cut.

Colour: full flat colour on the printed faces, four inks, no shading — the folk art at the largest three masses only, the mural lid in full because it is the reward for finishing. Fold and cut furniture printed in a 30 % warm grey so it never competes.

The verso is the plate: assembly steps in exploded axonometric, the dimension table, and the sentence that makes the object make sense — `The real one was cut open with an angle grinder in 2006. Yours needs scissors.`

Annotation as poetry lives in the notes column, in the flat imperative voice a real fabrication drawing uses: `MATERIAL: 300 gsm. ORIGINAL: 1.2 mm STEEL.` · `FOLD ALL DASHED LINES AWAY FROM YOU.` · `CUT THE THREE APERTURES FIRST. THE DONOR VEHICLE ARRIVED WITHOUT THEM TOO.` · `THE UNDERSIDE IS INCLUDED FOR COMPLETENESS. NOBODY WILL EVER SEE IT.` · `DO NOT FIT AN ENGINE. THERE ISN'T ONE.` · `SCALE 1:24. THE ORIGINAL IS 4.065 m AND HAS NOT MOVED SINCE 2006.`

And the deep wrongness that makes this a subverted blueprint rather than a craft kit: a development drawing is an IMPOSSIBLE VIEW BY DEFINITION. No camera, no eye, no render can ever see all six faces of a solid at once at true scale. It is the one drafting form that shows you something reality withholds. Printing it as an object the reader can fold back into three dimensions closes that loop in the reader's hands.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Anticipation, then absorption, then possession. It is the only thing in this entire program that ASKS SOMETHING OF THE PERSON, and that is why it will be the one they keep. A poster is looked at; this is worked on — twenty minutes at a table with scissors, a parent and a child, and at the end there is a small red bus on the shelf that they made. The emotional mechanism is well understood and almost never used by restaurant brands: effort creates attachment. Nobody throws away a thing they built. And the notes carry a second, quieter feeling for the adult doing the cutting — 'THE UNDERSIDE IS INCLUDED FOR COMPLETENESS. NOBODY WILL EVER SEE IT' is a joke about thoroughness that is also a small confession of love for an object, and it is exactly what seventy-six revisions of measuring the underbody to ±10.5 mm actually feels like from the inside.

**FORMATS.** A2 (594 × 420 mm) single sheet at 1:24 — my arithmetic off STATE.md's L 4.065 m and W 1.7497 m puts the assembled model at 169 × 73 × 83 mm, which is a satisfying desk object and fits one A2 net comfortably.  ·  A3 pair (297 × 420 mm ×2) at 1:32 — assembled 127 mm long — as the restaurant giveaway. Cheaper, mails flat, fits a bill folder.  ·  A 'PLATE ONLY' variant: the identical development printed as a FLAT POSTER on paper, uncut, no tabs, with the fold lines kept as a graphic. This is the version that hangs on a wall and it is arguably the better poster of the two, because an unfolded object is a genuinely strange and beautiful shape.  ·  Screen-printed tea towel or bandana, 500 × 500 mm, development at 1:40 with the fold lines as the pattern's structure.  ·  1:12 collector version (339 mm assembled) on two A1 sheets, in the limited tier, with a die-cut and creased finish rather than scissors.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** THIS IS THE RISKY ONE AND I AM NOT SANDING IT DOWN. Two things make it uncopyable. First, YOU CANNOT UNFOLD SOMETHING YOU HAVE NOT MEASURED. A stock VW-bus seller has a traced silhouette; a development drawing requires the actual three-dimensional skin, panel by panel, at true scale — this program has 229 mesh objects, 376,383 vertices and zero non-manifold body edges, and this is the single artefact that could not exist without all of it. It is the one output that turns seventy-six revisions of measurement into something a customer can hold. Second, it is a form the merchandise market simply does not use: the saturated moves are sunset, surf, hippie, line art and blueprint, and every one of them is a picture. This is not a picture, it is a set of instructions.

IT ALSO CONTRADICTS THE PRIOR ART AND I AM PROPOSING IT ANYWAY. This is die-cut, it is for children at the restaurant, and it is meant to be kept by families — which is F18's territory — but it is NOT the F18 deliverable and must not be offered as one. F18's style is locked to 'cartoon with rendered depth' and its scene to 'nothing but the bus, die-cut tight, plus the sun and the papel picado'; this is flat technical drawing, contains no sun, and its whole subject is the drafting furniture F18's brief has no room for. It is a DIFFERENT ARTEFACT, it is therefore not gated by F18's 'build it after the model is done' trigger, and the proposal must say so in the same breath it is offered. A cautious team rejects it for three reasons — it looks like a craft kit rather than a premium brand object, it demands real production work nobody in this pipeline has done, and it can be assembled badly by the customer, which is a brand risk a poster does not carry. All three objections are correct. Ship it anyway: it is the only thing here that a family will still own in ten years.

**PRODUCTION.** CUT-PATH ENGINEERING IS THE WHOLE JOB and it is where this fails if it fails. At 1:24 the arch-to-tyre gap is 39.7 mm on the vehicle and therefore 1.65 mm on the sheet — below any safe cut minimum. Bridge it: the arches are cut, the tyres are printed. Rules to design to and to test on a proof, not to assume: no concave notch narrower than ~3 mm, no internal radius under ~1.5 mm, no tab under 4 mm, and every cut line at least 1.5 mm from the nearest printed edge for registration slop. The aperture corner radius at 1:24 is 2.3 mm, which is comfortably cuttable; the apertures themselves are 21.5 × 16.8 mm, which is generous.

DRAW THE FRONT PANEL'S ROUNDEL AS FLAT LINE ART, NOT AS A RENDER, and this is not an aesthetic preference — it is the escape hatch. At 1:24 the roundel is 11.7 mm on the sheet, which is well above the size at which its arrangement is judgeable, and the built mark is the owner's ninth reported defect: the pose-free shape IoU is 0.8528 against a control ceiling of 0.9465, and the built cells read 1.19× too round against the photograph. A development drawing legitimately draws marks as line rather than sampling them from a render, so the whole sheet ships correctly while the emblem remains open. Keep the drawn strokes near-parallel, which is what the photograph shows and what the model gets wrong.

THE DARTS ARE A FEATURE, NOT AN APOLOGY. The body is doubly curved — rake 17.75 mm/m, the V-swage, the crowned roof — so a true development is geometrically impossible. Put the darts in, label them, and print the note. A drawing that admits its own approximation is on-theme with the entire program.

Prototype in paper at 1:32 before anything is committed. Fold it yourself, badly, at a kitchen table, and see which tab tears. No amount of measurement substitutes for that; this project's own first rule is render it, crop it and look at it, and the paper equivalent is cut it, fold it and look at it.

DO NOT put a sun on it, and do not put papel picado on it. Both belong to F18's locked scene and the owner has explicitly deferred the papel-picado question until the sticker is actually being built. Leave them out and leave them unasked.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** A UV UNWRAP AND A TEXTURE BAKE — this is the load-bearing job and nothing like it exists. The flank folk art is BOX-PROJECTED in the shader (t1_mats: swirl.png / swirl_b.png with projection='BOX'), which means it has no UV coordinates at all and cannot survive being laid flat. Every painted surface that appears on the net must first be baked to a UV set: folk art, nose art, the Calidad decal, the wordmark, the mural lid. The mural lid and the Calidad decal are already conformed panels and are easy; the box-projected flank art is the real work.  ·  AN EXPORT PATH. `grep -rn "export_scene|wm.obj_export|gltf|usd_export|fbx"` returns nothing across the entire tree. Without one, the panels cannot be flattened in any tool that knows how to develop a surface. Add glTF and USD behind one switch, alongside documenting T1_SAVE. State the honest ceiling: 0 non-manifold body edges is encouraging but says nothing about UV integrity, material translation, or how the 5,186 ngons behave through an exporter — triangulate on export and check the artwork's islands survive.  ·  PER-PANEL ORTHOGRAPHIC CAPTURES as the fallback route if the unwrap proves expensive: render each face square-on through its own orthographic camera and assemble the net from six flat images. Cruder, loses the darts' honesty, but it is achievable this month and the unwrap is not.  ·  THE CLOSED CONFIGURATION, WHICH HAS NEVER BEEN RENDERED IN SEVENTY-SIX REVISIONS. The net is a shut vehicle, and shutting it is blocked by the lid's width-angle coupling (LID_W = (LID_X0 − LID_X1)/LID_ASPECT/sin(a) → 3.47 m at 20°, 6.84 m at 10°, division by zero at 0°). Freeze LID_W behind T1_LID_WCOUPLE with a guard row that watches the coupled build fail first, and give the `_top.co.y > LID_Y_HINGE + 0.010` assert a closed-end branch. Then render the shut bus and LOOK AT IT — that picture does not exist and every decision here depends on it.  ·  THE UNDERSIDE IS BUILT AND HAS NEVER BEEN LOOKED AT. STATE.md carries underpan, under_close_a, under_close_f and chassis_rail ×2, with 'underbody proudness: worst −55.8 mm ... INBOARD of the skin everywhere' and 'worst intrusion +10.5 mm at (x +1.897, y −0.03) over 1400 perimeter station(s)'. Render it from below before drawing it. This is the sheet's best joke and it must be true.  ·  THE NOSE FIXTURES ARE UNGRADEABLE FROM BEHIND — STATE.md says of both headlamp bowls 'NOT GRADEABLE -- all 64 back-face rays fall through the headlamp bore; no skin behind it', i.e. there is an open hole in the nose skin behind each lamp. That is invisible in every rendered view and it becomes a visible hole in a development of the skin. Either close it or design the net so the lamps are separate glued-on parts.  ·  NO EMBLEM WORK, NO GLOSS WORK, NO RED CORRECTION REQUIRED. A flat-ink development inherits none of the render's photoreal defects: the gloss deficit (render spread 0.412 of the photograph against a 0.60 bar) does not exist in flat colour, and the salmon red is replaced by a declared ink. This artefact routes around the two hardest open fidelity problems in the project entirely.

**SCREENER.** Intellectually the deepest idea: a development is an impossible view by definition — no camera can see six faces at true scale — so printing it and letting the reader fold it back closes a loop in their hands. The only concept that ASKS something of the person, and effort creates attachment. 'NOBODY WILL EVER SEE IT' and the angle-grinder line are both earned. Correctly distinguishes itself from F18 without claiming its slot. Fatal risk is cost, not taste: it needs a UV unwrap of art that has no UVs, an export path that does not exist, and the closed configuration nobody has rendered in 76 revisions.

---

## NUNCA VOLVIÓ A CAMINAR — the ex-voto — A hand-painted oil-on-salvaged-tin retablo giving thanks that the engine was scrapped: the bus painted naively, the removed flat-four and transaxle floating in a nimbus, and a hand-lettered inscription in Spanish thanking the combi for never running again.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `t13-2`

**THE IMAGE.** THE OBJECT: a Mexican ex-voto / retablo. Oil on a salvaged sheet of tin, roughly 250 x 350 mm, portrait. Scene above, ruled inscription band below — the form's fixed architecture.

THE TIN IS NOT NEW. Rusted at the edges, one corner dented, two nail holes at the top, the galvanising showing through where the paint went thin. The paint sits ON it, proud, with visible brush ridges. The sheet is 0.5 mm thick and slightly bowed. Everything about the substrate says salvage, which is what the bus is.

THE SCENE, upper two thirds: the bus, three-quarter from the SERVING side, painted NAIVELY on purpose — perspective slightly wrong, wheels drawn as full ellipses where they should be occluded, the counter drawn flat-on while the body is at an angle, no cast shadow, a flat chalky sky and a horizontal band of ochre ground. Ex-voto perspective is always a little wrong and that is the form's signature, not a defect to fix. The body is over-saturated vermillion; the folk art is laid in with a rigger in three strokes per motif; the Senor Tacombi lockup is hand-lettered and slightly too big for its space, which is how sign painters actually get it wrong.

THE ONE MIRACLE ELEMENT, and it is the whole tenderness of the piece: in a real ex-voto the upper corner carries the saint who intervened, in a cloud. Ours has the cloud. Inside it, painted small and flat like relics in a rayed ochre nimbus, are THE ENGINE AND THE GEARBOX — a flat-four and a transaxle, floating. The parts that were removed, ascended.

NO SAINT. NO VIRGIN. NO CROSS. NO DIVINE FIGURE. The gratitude is addressed to the bus and to the customers. That is a deliberate line in the work itself, not a caption, and it is what keeps the form homage rather than costume.

THE INSCRIPTION, lower third, ruled band on chalky cream, hand-lettered in a condensed brush script — black, first line in red. This is the exact register the vehicle already carries on its own counter fascia ('267 / ELIZABETH STREET / NUEVA YORK' above 'Gracias por su Preferencia.' in red cursive):

  'En 1963 esta combi salio de fabrica. La compramos por tres mil. Le faltaban tres ventanas. La trajimos rodando hasta Playa del Carmen y alli se paro para siempre. Le quitamos el motor y le abrimos el costado. DAMOS GRACIAS PORQUE NUNCA VOLVIO A CAMINAR.'

English beneath, small, lighter weight, ending on the line that carries the whole thing: '...WE GIVE THANKS THAT IT NEVER RAN AGAIN.' Signed the way ex-votos are signed — place and year, lower corner: 'Playa del Carmen'.

PALETTE: limited and slightly sour. Vermillion, ochre, chalky cream, a dirty cobalt for the sky band, manganese black for the lettering, and the tin's own grey showing through at the wear. Deliberately less saturated and less clean than the render. No gloss anywhere except the varnish, and the varnish should be uneven and slightly yellowed.

LIGHT FOR THE PHOTOGRAPH: single hard source at 45 degrees from the left, warm but not candle-coloured. Let the tin's bow put one soft highlight down the middle and let the dents throw real shadow. Shoot it a degree or two off square, against plaster, so it reads as an object photographed rather than a file exported.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** This is the only piece in the programme that turns the origin story into an act of GRATITUDE rather than a founder anecdote. Every VW-bus print ever made sells freedom, motion and escape. This one gives thanks for a breakdown. A person in the queue reads 'we give thanks that it never ran again', smiles at the joke, and then a beat later feels the other thing underneath it — that staying put is a decision, that the best thing that happened to this object was that it stopped. It is also the only piece whose payload is WORDS, which means it is the only 250 mm object in the programme that can carry the entire brand on its own. The engine-and-gearbox nimbus is the detail people will describe to each other; it means nothing to a stranger and everything to anyone who has read the panel.

**FORMATS.** THE ORIGINAL: one hand-painted tin per restaurant, commissioned from a working retablero, each one different, each hung above its own counter. Never reproduced identically — the form is one-off by definition and a run of identical ex-votos is a contradiction.  ·  Retail reproduction: VITREOUS ENAMEL ON STEEL, 200 x 280 mm. This is the industrially honest translation of oil-on-tin — it reads as a bar sign or a road sign, it is heavy and cold in the hand, and it is genuinely permanent. Enamel is the correct answer here and a giclee print is not.  ·  Postcard, 100 x 150 mm, given free with the bill. Highest emotional value per unit cost in the entire programme, and the item people actually keep — F18's own test ('should spark joy and be something families keep') applied to an adult.  ·  A5 riso print, three colours — vermillion, ochre, black on cream stock. Riso's misregistration and flat inks match the palette's own limitation; it is the cheap format that does not cheapen the idea.  ·  Enamel pin, 30 mm, hard enamel, gold plate: the cloud with the engine and the gearbox inside it, nimbus and all. No vehicle, no wordmark, no VW mark. A mark that is illegible to strangers and complete to anyone who read the panel.  ·  NOT APPAREL. Putting a devotional form on a t-shirt is where this tips from homage into costume. Hold the line.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** There is no VW-bus ex-voto. More to the point, there is no VW-bus merchandise anywhere that is ABOUT the engine being gone — the entire category sells the bus as a vehicle, and this is the only piece that treats it as a building, which is exactly what Tacombi's own account says it is: 'The engine was scrapped, transmission sold, seats swapped for cooking equipment, and the roof was reeled back to produce a taco serving stand' (SPEC.md §0.1, graded S). It also escapes the category's deepest trap, which is not visual but SEMANTIC: the split-screen T1 silhouette is the shape the American counterculture market wore out, and no crop or palette can fully escape it — but a devotional Mexican folk form cannot be misread as Woodstock in any light. And there is a material loop underneath it that makes the whole thing cohere: the vehicle IS a hand-painted metal panel. The ex-voto is a hand-painted metal panel. Same craft, different scale. The bus is already an ex-voto, four metres long.

**PRODUCTION.** THE RISK, NAMED HONESTLY, BECAUSE THIS IS THE ONE A CAUTIOUS TEAM KILLS: the ex-voto is a devotional form. Using it commercially can read as appropriation or as flippancy, and that objection is legitimate rather than squeamish. Three mitigations, all of which change the WORK and not just the caption: (1) no divine figure, no saint, no cross, no Virgin — the nimbus holds machine parts and the thanks are addressed to the bus and the customers; (2) the pieces are COMMISSIONED FROM Mexican retableros and credited to them by name on the reverse, on the postcard and in any press; (3) the inscription is written first-person-plural by the people who actually did it, not by an agency. If those three conditions cannot be met, do not make it. A cautious team would kill this at the word 'nimbus'. I would not — but I would not fake any of the three.

FACTS THAT MUST BE CONFIRMED WITH THE OWNER BEFORE ANYTHING IS LETTERED, because lettering is permanent and this repository's own sources disagree: the AMOUNT (SPEC.md §0.1 grades it S at $3,000 — DOLLARS, so 'tres mil pesos' would be wrong; I have written 'por tres mil' precisely to avoid committing a currency), the YEAR (SPEC records 'Opened 6 February 2006' with the bus bought in Mexico City, while one public search summary says founded in Mexico City in 2005 — and SPEC has its own internal tension between the buy date and the open date), and whether he wants the sum stated at all. Do not let a web summary set a number that gets painted onto a hundred tins.

CASTING THE PAINTER: working retableros exist in Guanajuato and Mexico City. Brief them with reference and with the inscription, then get out of the way — the naivety of the perspective is the craft's, not something to art-direct into existence. If the drawing comes back 'correct', it has failed.

ENAMEL SPEC: two-coat vitreous enamel on 1.2 mm steel, rolled edge, single mounting hole top centre. Enamel cannot hold fine gradient, so the reproduction art must be separated to flat colours — which the palette already is. Proof one before the run; the vermillion is the colour most likely to shift in the kiln.

PHOTOGRAPHY: this piece must be photographed, never rendered. A render of a distressed tin panel is a lie about the one property the idea rests on.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** ALMOST NONE, AND THAT IS THE POINT — this is a hand-painted object and the 3D asset is REFERENCE, not source. What it needs is one good three-quarter reference from the serving side for the painter to work from.  ·  THAT REFERENCE DOES NOT CURRENTLY EXIST AS A CAMERA. AUDIT_rev43.md locks the viewpoint at '18 degree front three-quarter from the serving side, eye height 1.55 m' and studio.views() has no such entry. hero34f is loc=(12.20, 8.55, 3.55) tgt=(-0.15, 0.00, 1.34) — azimuth atan(8.55/12.35) = 34.7 degrees at 3.55 m camera height, right side but wrong angle and wrong eye height; front34 is on -Y, the wrong side entirely (STATE.md: 'open serving apertures on +Y: 3'). Add a `sticker18` view. It is a few lines, and 1.55 m is also the reference photograph's OWN camera height, which REF_MEASUREMENTS.md derives independently: 'a pinhole model with camera height ~ 1.55 m, horizon y ~ 340, puts the wall 1.2x and the man 0.9x the bus distance — physically sane.'  ·  DO NOT HAND THE PAINTER A RENDER OF THE NOSE. This is the one concept whose hero view puts the roundel in frame at large size. It is the owner's ninth report — P2 0.8528 against P1b's 0.9465, and C8: 'photo 3.39, built 2.84 -- the built cells are 1.19x TOO ROUND. Four fat wedges meeting at the centre IS the X the owner has reported five times.' Give them ref_playa_34.png for the roundel and the render for the proportions, and say which is which on the reference sheet. This is also the reason this concept is the one most exposed to F191, and it should be stated in the proposal rather than discovered later.  ·  FIX THE SCRIPT'S MISSING KEYLINE ANYWAY — it does not block this piece (the painter letters by hand) but it is the cheapest visible improvement in the whole inventory and it blocks everything else. SPEC §3 requires the wordmark 'SILVER with a dark keyline and drop shadow'; measured on tex/senor.png (4096 x 1738 RGBA), among pixels with alpha > 200 the 1st-percentile luma is 168.3 and the fraction below luma 80 is 0.0000 — the shipped master contains NO dark ink at all. script_gen.py is NOT called by build.py, so regenerate by hand or the render silently keeps the old texture.  ·  VW TRADEMARK: this piece carries a visible painted roundel at large scale, which is the highest-exposure element in the programme, on a devotional object that will be photographed and shared. That is an owner-and-counsel question before any run, not a design decision. Note the partial mitigation the vehicle itself supplies — on this bus the roundel is painted body-red relief, not reproduced chrome (SPEC §3: 'painted RED on the cream nose, V above W, pressed relief not chrome').

**SCREENER.** 'We give thanks that it never ran again' is the best line here, and the engine-and-gearbox ascended in the nimbus is the detail people repeat out loud; the material loop (the bus IS a hand-painted metal panel) is earned, and it escapes the category semantically where no crop can. Appropriation risk is real but the three mitigations are structural, not caption-level, which is the correct answer. Docked for tin-retablo pastiche having visible precedent in design-land, and for a hero view that puts the owner's ninth-report roundel in frame at size.

---

## The Second String — Two continuous strokes and nothing else: the festoon tracing the roof, and the queue drawn at exactly the same weight below it — the bulbs end at the tail board, the line of people runs off the edge of the frame.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `t15-2`

**THE IMAGE.** ONE LINE DRAWING. TWO STROKES. NO VEHICLE.

UPPER STROKE — THE FESTOON: starts at the nose, runs the full length of the drip rail, wraps the rear roof dome, and drops along the propped tail board's free edge, ending at the board's tip. Bulbs are small filled circles set on the stroke at their built pitch. This run is real, not invented: STATE.md carries `bulb_string | 1` plus `tb_bulbs | 1` and `tb_bulbflex | 1`, so the string genuinely continues onto the propped board, and t1_detail records that the drip-rail run sits OUTSIDE the skin, ~55 mm above the aperture heads. The stroke's shape therefore describes the vehicle's whole silhouette without drawing a single panel.

LOWER STROKE — THE QUEUE: a single line of people at the SAME weight, the SAME colour and the SAME degree of abstraction. Heads are filled circles at the same diameter as the bulbs; bodies are the connecting stroke. Read at a glance, the two are indistinguishable in kind: two strings of warm dots, one above the bus and one beside it. That equivalence is the entire idea and it must survive squinting from three metres — if the queue reads as figures rather than as lights, the drawing has failed.

THE VEHICLE: not drawn, or drawn as the faintest cream ghost at 8–10 % — enough to explain why the upper stroke has that shape and no more. The bus is the reason the lines are where they are, and it is not the subject.

COMPOSITION: extremely wide, 8:1 to 12:1. Both strokes run the full width. The festoon STOPS at the tail board's tip. The queue runs OFF the right edge of the frame. That asymmetry is the warmth and the joke — the lights have an end, the line does not.

PALETTE: two colours, hard stop. NIGHT: warm amber-gold on a near-black warm ground (#1A0E0A). DAY: dark maroon #501010 on cream sRGB(206,208,200). No tone, no gradient, no glow — the glow is implied by the colour and by the black around it, never by a blur, because a blur is the one thing that does not survive to 12 mm or to 32 px.

MOTION, 6 s, and it is the best logo animation available to this brand: the festoon ignites left to right, bulb by bulb, at the built pitch, hard-stepped. As the last bulb lights, the queue's heads begin lighting in the same order and the same rhythm, slightly slower. Hold, both lines lit. Then ONE head at the counter end goes out and detaches (served, walking off). The whole line advances one place. Loop. Mechanically this is two `stroke-dashoffset` animations on two SVG paths — under 10 KB, no raster, no render, runs everywhere.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** It flatters the customer instead of the vehicle, which nothing else in this program does. The second stroke IS the reader — you are in the drawing, and you are drawn as a light. What it says without a word is that a queue outside a taco truck is the same warm signal as a string of bulbs: a full line is not a wait, it is the evidence that the place is on. Anyone who has stood in one recognises the reframe instantly, and it is a kind thing to say to someone standing in one.

The last beat carries the whole feeling: a light goes out at the front, and everyone moves up. That is what a good queue feels like from inside it, and it takes one second of animation to say.

**FORMATS.** LAPTOP / HEM STRIP STICKER, 200 × 12 mm, one ink on clear or matte white PVC. A continuous line is the only graphic form that is honest at 12 mm — no reduction penalty, nothing to fill in, no minimum feature to lose.  ·  SIGN ABOVE THE ACTUAL COUNTER, 1200 × 150 mm, amber on dark, printed or routed, hung so the drawn festoon sits directly under the real one. Same trick as the counter vinyl: artwork and subject in one field of view.  ·  FLOOR DECALS IN THE RESTAURANT — 150 mm amber circles marking the queue positions, spaced at the same pitch as the bulbs overhead. This is the piece leaving the wall and becoming furniture: the queue is literally drawn on the floor as a string of lights, and every customer completes the artwork by standing on it. It is the strongest version of the idea and by far the cheapest to produce.  ·  SVG LOOP, 1600 × 200, under 10 KB — site header, email header, in-store screen. Two paths, `stroke-dashoffset`, no library, no video file, no compression artefacts at any zoom.  ·  FAVICON / APP ICON, 32 px — the two strokes collapse to two dotted lines and still read as two strings. Almost nothing in this program survives 32 px; this does, by construction.  ·  EMBROIDERY, chain-stitch or bean-stitch, 90 × 10 mm on a cap back or a sleeve seam. A running stitch, so the ~1.2 mm satin-column floor does not apply and the line survives at that width — the only motif from the whole vehicle that embroiders honestly at that size.  ·  LONG-FORMAT PRINT, 1200 × 150 mm or 1600 × 200 mm, two spot inks on uncoated. Cheap to print, impossible to ignore on a wall because nothing else is that shape.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** Minimal line art IS a named saturated trope in this market — the survey found "minimalist illustrator styles featuring trace monotone and black lines" and "a vintage minimalist line drawing style" as standing categories. So this idiom is occupied. What is not occupied is what the line is OF. Every one of those listings draws the vehicle's outline. This draws the vehicle's LIGHTS and its CUSTOMERS, puts the vehicle itself in the background of its own line drawing at 8 % opacity, and makes the crowd the subject. The trope's own language, turned against its own content.

It also structurally cannot be mistaken for escape imagery. A queue is the least aspirational thing you can draw and the most communal — the exact inversion of a lone bus at sunset with a surfboard on the roof. And a queue is only possible around a vehicle that has stopped, which is this brand's founding fact and no competitor's.

One further advantage: the festoon is the element the RENDER currently fails at — F144 stands, "THE FESTOON BULBS READ AS PEARLS, NOT LIGHTS." A drawn line can be right before the 3D is. This is the only concept in the set that is better as a drawing than as a render, so it ships while the model is still under its holds.

**PRODUCTION.** THE RISK HERE IS NOT THE IDEA, IT IS THE ABSTRACTION LEVEL, and it must be resolved on paper before anything is built: if the queue reads as PEOPLE the drawing becomes a cartoon and dies; if it reads as LIGHTS it works. Prototype at three sizes — 12 mm, 150 mm, 1200 mm — on the first day, pin them up, and squint from three metres. The head-circle diameter relative to the bulb diameter is the single variable that decides it.

DO NOT ASSERT A BULB COUNT OR PITCH. I could not find a published figure for either and I am not going to invent one. Measure the built string's bulb spacing off the mesh and draw the dots to that; if the mesh reading is ambiguous, say so and choose a pitch as an admitted design decision. This project's own tyre-tread finding is the cautionary case — `TREAD_LUGS = 64  # DECLARED, inside the MEASURED bracket 48..84` and the probe REFUSES to publish a count at all. A drawn festoon at poster scale publishes a countable claim; make sure it is one we can stand behind or make sure it is labelled a drawing.

NO OWNER RULING ON ANIMATION EXISTS ANYWHERE IN THIS RECORD. A grep for 'animat' across every .md and .txt returns one file and one hit — a persona description in PANEL_rev61.md. So the motion half of this concept is ungrounded work and must be PUT TO HIM as multiple choice with the loop attached, not inferred from the promotional-material ruling.

NOT THE F18 STICKER (different scene, different style, and F18's trigger has not fired), and the floor-decal application is outside every ruling in the record — propose it as new.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** EXTRACT THE FESTOON PATH. It is currently ONE merged object — t1_detail joins it as `join([ob, flex], "bulb_string")` — which is fine for tracing a single continuous stroke and fatal for anything per-bulb. There is no 3D export path in this tree at all (a grep for export_scene / obj_export / gltf / usd_export / fbx returns nothing; the only persistence is build.py's `if os.environ.get("T1_SAVE"): bpy.ops.wm.save_as_mainfile(...)`, which appears in ZERO handoff documents), so the path must be traced off a rendered `side` frame or authored by hand from the mesh coordinates.  ·  IF THE CHASE ANIMATION IS EVER DRIVEN FROM THE RENDER RATHER THAN FROM SVG: keep the bulbs addressable instead of collapsing them into one mesh with one material, behind an ablation switch so nothing regresses. `T1_BULB_STR` and `T1_BULB_BASEV` already exist as the string's ablations and are the right place to hang it.  ·  F144 IS THE STANDING DEFECT AND THIS CONCEPT ROUTES AROUND IT RATHER THAN FIXING IT — "THE FESTOON BULBS READ AS PEARLS, NOT LIGHTS." Say which you are using in every deliverable: a drawn line owes nothing to the render, but the moment a frame shows RENDERED bulbs it inherits the defect. If the piece ever wants a photoreal night frame, F144 becomes required work and the emission needs to clear post.py's `BLOOM_THR = 0.94` to actually flare.  ·  THE FRONT OF THE VEHICLE IS UNDER-BUILT BY ONE APERTURE, and it matters if the stroke ever wraps the nose. t1_detail builds the white bobble fringe on the three serving bays only (`for i, (xr, xf) in enumerate(S.BAYS)`; STATE.md `fringe | 3`), while ref_playa_34.png shows an unmistakable row of white balls running the full inner edge of the WINDSCREEN aperture. Found and recorded here; it is in no register row I could locate.  ·  NOTHING ELSE. This is the only concept of the three that ships with the model exactly as it stands today — no alpha render, no new camera, no relight, no emblem, no resolution ceiling, no stitch chain. It is two vector paths. Ship it first.

**SCREENER.** Sharpest idea and highest idea-to-cost ratio: two strokes at identical weight so the festoon and the queue are indistinguishable in kind, the lights ending and the line running off frame, and one head going out at the front so everyone advances. Kindest piece here — it flatters the person standing in the queue. Survives 32 px, 12 mm and embroidery by construction, routes around F144 rather than needing it fixed, ships with zero model work. Docked because minimal line art is a named saturated idiom and the still is much less than the motion beat.

---

## TAMAÑO REAL — the room becomes the bus — Stop printing pictures of the bus at arbitrary sizes and print ONE PART at 1:1, mounted at the height it actually sits, so the wall you are standing at becomes the flank you ordered from in Playa in 2006.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `t16-0`

**THE IMAGE.** The hero artefact has no bus in it. It is a band of three rounded openings — 515.5 × 403.0 mm each, corner radius 55.0 mm, centres 625.0 and 645.0 mm apart so the two pillars between them are 109.5 mm and 129.5 mm and DELIBERATELY UNEQUAL — cut as an optically clear window cling and mounted on the storefront glass with its sill line at 1307 mm above the floor. Inside each opening: nothing. The street, the room, whoever is standing behind it. A row of white bobble-fringe scallops runs the inner edge of all three. Below the middle opening, in 8 pt condensed caps, one line: TAMAÑO REAL · ESCALA 1:1. The palette is one ink (white) on air. Companion pieces continue the logic: a 700 × 700 mm square print of ONE WHEEL at true size — 664.9 mm tyre, 274.0 mm cream cap, black sidewall, shot dead front-on so the tread is edge-on and no lug count is ever published — on uncoated matte, so it reads as the object rather than as a photograph of it; and one physical tail board, 711.0 mm of chord at 38.0° above horizontal, cream face, red edge stripe, dark navy lower stripe, with a live bulb string along the outer edge, bracketed to the wall above the counter. No motion. The whole piece is the handbrake.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Recognition with a small physical shock. A person walks up to order, and their hands come up to a sill at 1307 mm — high, higher than a bar, the way you reach up at a truck window — and their body remembers something their eyes have not been told. Every other piece of merchandise in this category asks you to LOOK at a bus. This one puts you where a customer stood. And the second beat, for the person who leans in: the three pillars are not the same width. Nobody designs that. It could only have been measured. That is the whole feeling — you are touching a fact, not a graphic.

**FORMATS.** Aperture band window cling, 1785.5 × 403.0 mm overall (three 515.5 × 403.0 openings, r 55.0, pillars 109.5 / 129.5 mm). Optically clear polyester, SECOND-SURFACE mount on the inside face of the glass so it survives weather and cleaning. Sill line at 1307 mm AFF. It is drawn from constants, so it is pure vector — no render, no resolution ceiling, no dependency on anything in the delivery chain.  ·  Single-aperture menu frame, 515.5 × 403.0 mm, routed 6 mm MDF painted cream with the bobble fringe applied, hung at 1307 mm. The day's menu goes inside the hole. Physical rather than printed because the piece's whole argument is that it is the real size — a printed rectangle claiming to be a hole undercuts it.  ·  1:1 wheel print, 700 × 700 mm square, 300 gsm uncoated rag, no lamination. Uncoated on purpose: at true size a gloss stock reads as a photograph of a wheel and a matte one reads as the wheel. Front-on only.  ·  1:1 tail board, fabricated: 711.0 mm chord, 38.0° from horizontal, cream / red edge / dark lower edge, live festoon along the free edge. One per site. The only object in the program that is the vehicle rather than an image of it.  ·  Pocket card, 105 × 148 mm, 300 gsm uncoated, one per table: the same three apertures reduced to 1:8 with a scale bar and the sill height. It is the instruction manual for the wall, and it is the take-home.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** Every VW-bus artefact on the market is a picture of a whole bus at whatever size the substrate happened to be. This is a fragment at exactly one size, and the size IS the content. It cannot be copied because it cannot be eyeballed — 515.5 mm, 403.0 mm, r 55.0, and the unequal 109.5 / 129.5 pillars come off `t1_shell.py`'s `BAY_W = 0.5155  # equal, measured`, `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550` and `BAY_CX = (0.6720, 0.0470, -0.5980)`, and nobody else has spent seventy-five revisions measuring this vehicle. It also carries zero VW content: no roundel, no bumper, no bus silhouette, nothing Volkswagen ever pressed. The three holes are the CONVERSION, and SPEC.md §0.1 grades the reason for them at source level — the $3,000 donor bus was 'missing three of its windows'. The signature feature is an accident of cheapness, and this piece is the only way to hand someone that accident at full size.

**PRODUCTION.** The aperture band is the one deliverable in this whole program that ships with no render, no Blender, no alpha channel and no large-format chain — it is drawn from source constants and it could be at a printer this week. Mount second-surface and specify a low-tack clear polyester, not a cast vinyl, or the pillars at 109.5 mm will tunnel at the corners. On the wheel print: NEVER show the tread. `t1_detail.py` carries `TREAD_LUGS = 64  # DECLARED, inside the MEASURED bracket 48..84 (F308)` and the probe refuses to publish a count at all; at 1:1 the lug pitch is 32.6 mm and a customer can count them, so a side-on 1:1 wheel would publish a specific checkable claim the project cannot defend. Front-on kills it. On the hubcap badge: STATE.md says in terms 'hubcap badge is SELF-CONSISTENCY ONLY -- CAP_EMBLEM_WFRAC has never been compared to a frame; see PHOTOS_WANTED item 7' — so either close item 7 with one square-on hubcap photograph or print the cap without its glyph. Printing an unverified proportion at maximum size is exactly the wrong direction. Same discipline on the fringe: `t1_detail.py` records the ball diameter and pitch as INFERRED at roughly 2 px in the reference, so draw the fringe and never dimension it.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** NOTHING for the aperture band, the menu frame or the pocket card — all three come off constants (BAY_W, BAY_CX, BAY_R, Z_SILL/Z_HEAD, whose above-ground values are 1.307 and 1.710) and off STATE.md's 'bay widths 0.516 0.515 0.516'. Ship them independent of the render pipeline entirely.  ·  Run T1_ALPHA=1 once for the wheel die-cut. Every frame in out/ today is PNG colortype 2 with no alpha, and deliver.py refuses them (C1 FAILED, 'PACKAGE IS NOT TRUSTWORTHY'). The switch has not been exercised since rev 62.  ·  DO NOT route that frame through post.py — it reads rgba[..., :3] and writes three channels, so the silhouette is destroyed silently.  ·  Add a front-on wheel camera. studio.views() has detail_f (the nose) and no wheel view at all.  ·  Close PHOTOS_WANTED item 7 — one hubcap, square on and close — or drop the badge from the 1:1 print.  ·  Nothing here needs the emblem, the nose, the gloss or the red to be fixed first, which is why it can ship under F191's hold: it is not the delivery render and it contains no part of the vehicle the owner has an open report against.

**SCREENER.** The only concept whose second read is a verifiable FACT rather than a caption — the 109.5/129.5 mm unequal pillars compute exactly off BAY_CX/BAY_W and are unfakeable authorship; printing one part at 1:1 at 1307 mm AFF is a strategic move, not a graphic, and it is bodily rather than visual. Best production discipline in the set (refuses the tread because TREAD_LUGS is DECLARED, refuses the hubcap glyph because STATE.md says self-consistency only). Held off 9 because the hero is superb and the companions — 1:1 wheel print, fabricated tail board — are ordinary.

## NUEVA YORK · PLAYA DEL CARMEN — The bus already carries its own destination board naming both ends of the route — print the board, not the bus.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `t17-0`

**THE IMAGE.** A single hand-painted rectangular sign, flat and dead frontal, filling the sheet edge to edge with a 25 mm cream border. No vehicle, no map, no road, no horizon. Composition, read straight off ref_nolita_doorshut.jpg: a cream-and-gold frame; along the TOP RAIL, three elements at equal type size and equal visual weight — 'NUEVA YORK' hard left, 'TACOMBI' centred in shadowed tricolour display caps (green/white/red, each letter carrying a hard offset drop shadow down-right), 'PLAYA DEL CARMEN' hard right; down BOTH JAMBS, 'BIENVENIDOS' set vertically in red with a small gold five-point star above and below; along the BASE RAIL, '...NDA NOLITA ★' in red. The centre is a matte black slate. In the photograph it is chalked 'TACOS DE HOY' with a price list. In the poster it carries the ROUTE instead, chalked in the same loose white hand: three lines, 'CIUDAD DE MÉXICO · 2006' / 'PLAYA DEL CARMEN' / 'NUEVA YORK', with a chalked arrow between them and nothing else. Palette is four inks and no gradient: warm off-white cream ground (t1_mats CREAM = sRGB 206,208,200, warmed toward the ivory the belt band reads at, #D4BDA7); the declared brand red; folk-art ochre-gold (t1_mats GOLD linear 0.86/0.54/0.06) for the frame keyline, the scallops and the stars; and a warm near-black for the slate — a soft, chalk-dusty black, never a printing black. Light: NONE. This is an object printed flat, not a photograph of an object. It is authored as vector, so it has no render in it at all and no lighting decision to make. Optional second version for the enamel-steel edition: reverse-print onto steel so the object IS the thing it depicts. Aspect: author at the board's own measured proportion — the closest live figure in the tree is t1_shell.LID_ASPECT = 1.7130, and it must be checked against the photograph before it is used, not assumed.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Recognition, then a quiet swell of pride of place. A person who has eaten at either counter sees the OTHER city's name on the board, at the same size, in the same hand — and the claim lands without a word of copy: this is not a Mexican restaurant in New York, it is one place with two addresses, and neither is the branch. The 'BIENVENIDOS' running down both sides does the rest; it is the most generous word on the object and it is repeated twice, in Spanish, to a New York street. For anyone who has been the person arriving, that is a direct hit.

**FORMATS.** Poster, A2 420×594 portrait and A1 594×841 — but note this piece is DRAWN VECTOR, so it is not gated by the render chain's resolution ceiling at all (nothing above 1600×1100 exists on disk; the stitched chain is proven once at 3840×2640). This is the only concept of the three that can print at any size next week.  ·  Enamel-on-steel sign, 300 × 175 mm and 600 × 350 mm — reverse-printed, rolled edge, four screen passes. The substrate IS the referent: this is what a real route board is made of. Highest-margin object in the set and the one people mount on a wall for good.  ·  Bandana / pañuelo, 550 × 550 mm, board centred on a folk-art ground with the rosette repeat filling the corners. Three inks on natural cotton.  ·  Woven hem label and inside-neck tag, 40 × 12 mm, carrying just 'NUEVA YORK · PLAYA DEL CARMEN' as a two-city rule — the insider mark, silent on the outside face.  ·  Animated title card, 1080×1080 and 1920×1080, 3–4 s: the frame and rails paint on first, then the chalk writes the three route lines in real chalk cadence with dust. Loops to a held board.  ·  A6 105 × 148 mm card dropped in the taco box, board on the face, the SPEC §0.1 origin sentence on the reverse.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** The entire VW-bus route category draws one of two things: a map with a dotted line, or a bus on a road at sunset. This draws neither, and contains no vehicle at all. What it draws is a real Mexican transport convention — a combi carries a hand-painted destination board saying where it runs — which is the WORKING-CLASS pesero reading of this body shape rather than the American counterculture one, and it is the reading the brand's own name commits to ('taco' + 'combi'). Nobody in the category is making that move because nobody else's bus has the board. Ours does, on the object, hand-lettered. Two secondary advantages that fall out for free: it carries zero VW roundel and zero bus silhouette, so it has no trademark or trade-dress content whatsoever; and it passes the logo-removal test outright — strike the word 'TACOMBI' from the centre and the two city names still tell you exactly what this is.

**PRODUCTION.** FOUR SPOT INKS, no process, no gradient, uncoated 300 gsm for the paper editions — which sidesteps every measured weakness of the render pipeline at once: the salmon red (flank_compare on out/r75_side.png reads render G/R 0.422 against the photograph's 0.114), the gloss deficit, and the 8-bit stitch chain. DECLARE THE RED as a design ruling with provenance; do NOT eyedrop it off a render and do NOT average the photographs — probe_rev71_red.py prints its own prohibition, 'the photograph's red G/R is 0.0149 .. 0.0344, a 2.3x span. DO NOT QUOTE A SINGLE FIGURE FOR IT.' The tree currently holds four unreconciled reds (t1_mats sRGB(196,49,36), lid_gen (159,45,22), cal_gen (214,46,30)); this piece needs one and the choice should be recorded, not smuggled. The tricolour wordmark must be REDRAWN, not traced — SPEC §10.10's own method, 'read the design at 4-5x magnification, redraw it faithfully at 2-4K, and then measure the result back against the crop.' Type: Spanish first and larger, English second and smaller, everywhere. Do not set the wordmark in a system face — the owner rejected exactly that by name at rev 8 ('That script i see on the p9 hero is NOT it'). CEILING, STATE IT ON THE ARTWORK BRIEF: the sole source frame is ref_nolita_doorshut.jpg at 480×320, the board spans roughly 220 × 130 px of it, and individual letterforms are 2–6 px. It is byte-identical to IMG_3840.jpeg (md5 f1b6f98c6a12b6e9ea0ec3edc68e945a), so this is n = 1 with no corroborating frame — a duplicate is not corroboration. Every letterform below display size is an INTERPRETATION and the brief must say so. AND FIX THE ADDRESS BEFORE ANYTHING PRINTS: SPEC §7 says '251 ELIZABETH STREET / NUEVA YORK'; I cropped the counter fascia at 4× and it reads 267 ELIZABETH STREET / NUEVA YORK over 'Gracias por su Preferencia.' The photograph wins. That is a live SPEC defect and a poster would reproduce it at a metre wide.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** NONE for the piece itself — and that is the point. It is drawn artwork, not a render, so it is unblocked by T1_ALPHA (unexercised since rev 62), by the delivery-render hold (F191/F193), by the emblem (his ninth report), by post.py's alpha destruction and by the unproven large-format chain. It can ship while every one of those is still open.  ·  IF it is ever wanted as a RENDER rather than as flat art, four things must be built and none exists: (a) tex/lidchalk.png — the chalkboard board is a DIFFERENT LIVERY STATE and there is no texture, no material binding and no generator for it; tex/lidsign.png is the 'La Santa' board, ruled restaurant furniture (F164) and gated behind T1_SIGNBOARD=1, which t1_shell.py itself says 'is not on the vehicle at all'; (b) a plain-red Nolita body state — build.py binds the folk art, the script and the Calidad decal unconditionally, so no livery switch exists; (c) rule 11 compliance — a chalkboard lid over a folk-art flank is a fake and the owner has ruled on it, so the render would need the whole Nolita state, not a swap; (d) lid_gen.py is NOT called by build.py, so any regeneration is a hand step or the render silently uses the old texture.  ·  ONE OWNER QUESTION, unrelated to this piece but surfaced by it and cheap to fold in: lid_gen.py says the mural is on the lid's OUTWARD face, SPEC §4 says the underside. Unresolved, and it decides whether a closed-lid render shows flowers or plain cream.  ·  REGISTER FIX, in the same edit: correct SPEC §7's '251 ELIZABETH STREET' to 267, cite the crop, and note it was found by looking rather than by a gate.

**SCREENER.** Culturally exact and emotionally precise — the pesero destination board is the WORKING-CLASS reading of this body shape rather than the American counterculture one, and putting both cities at equal size says 'neither is the branch' with no copy at all; ships next week as vector with no render dependency, and it caught a live SPEC defect (SPEC.md says 251 Elizabeth, the frame reads 267). Held off 9 because a reproduced enamel sign sits near a known merch trope and the source is n=1 at 480×320 with 2–6 px letterforms, so most of it is honest invention.

## USTED ESTÁ AQUÍ — The floor of the first restaurant, at 1:1 — The original bus's exact footprint inlaid into the floor of every Tacombi, so the queue stands inside the outline of the whole company as it was in 2006 — an image of the bus that contains no image of the bus.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `free0-2`

**THE IMAGE.** Not a picture. A drawing you stand in.

On the floor, at TRUE 1:1: a 4.065 × 1.750 m rectangle with the T1's real plan corners, laid in 6 mm brass strip flush with the terrazzo (permanent sites) or 3 mm printed anti-slip vinyl (leases and pop-ups). Inside it, only measured facts, drawn as a surveyor would draw them and nothing else:

— FOUR TYRE CONTACT PATCHES as filled ovals, at track 1.369 m front and 1.359 m rear, 2.400 m apart. They are the only solid shapes in the whole drawing, and they are where feet naturally land.
— THE WHEELBASE as a dimension line between the axle centres with the figure 2400 set in the break, engraved 8 mm caps.
— THE THREE SERVING BAYS marked on the +Y long edge as three 515.5 mm gaps in the brass, at their real centres — so the two solid pillars between them are 109.5 and 129.5 mm and the rhythm is visibly, correctly UNEQUAL. Stand on that edge and you are standing where the customer stood.
— THE COUNTER's cantilever as a hatched zone breaking the rectangle on the same edge, running past the tail.
— THE ENGINE BAY at the rear as a small empty rectangle, unfilled, with one engraved word inside it: VACÍO.
— AT THE TAIL, one line of 6 mm engraved type: 1963 · 4 065 × 1 750 mm · 7,11 m².

No colour. No red, no cream, no folk art, no wordmark, no bus. Brass on concrete, or dark grey on pale vinyl. The whole thing reads at a glance as a manhole plate or a survey monument, and only resolves when you notice you are inside it.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Vertigo, then affection. You are queueing in a 200-seat New York room and you look down and realise the entire company once fitted inside the rectangle you are standing in, and that you could walk it end to end in five paces. It converts the founding story from a paragraph on a website into a physical fact you occupy with your body. And it flatters nobody — there is no hero shot, no golden hour, no logo. It is the least promotional object imaginable and therefore the most persuasive. The last beat is the empty rectangle marked VACÍO at the tail: the engine went so the kitchen could come, and it is marked on the floor like a grave.

**FORMATS.** BRASS INLAY, 4.07 × 1.75 m, 6 mm × 3 mm half-hard brass strip set flush in terrazzo or poured concrete, engraved type filled with black wax. Brass because it is the counter's own metal, it takes foot polish rather than wear, and it is the only material that gets better with ten years of queue. Flagship and permanent sites only.  ·  PRINTED ANTI-SLIP FLOOR VINYL, same 4.07 × 1.75 m, R10 slip rating minimum, matte laminate, cut in four registered panels with 5 mm butt joints. Every other location and every pop-up, at roughly the cost of a single large print. This is how the idea scales to the whole estate in one week.  ·  1:20 LETTERPRESS PLAN, image 203 × 87 mm on a 300 × 400 mm sheet of 300 gsm cotton rag, deep impression, one dark ink plus blind-deboss on the tyre patches. The framed take-home. Letterpress because the drawing is all rule and no tone — it is the one artefact in this program that is genuinely better as an impression than as ink.  ·  LINEN TEA TOWEL, 1:12, image 339 × 146 mm on a 500 × 700 mm woven cloth. A floor plan on a tea towel is an old, good, slightly nerdy object type (people buy building plans as tea towels) and it lands in the exact deadpan register the brand's shop copy already uses.  ·  DIE-CUT BRASS BOOKMARK / KEY FOB, the footprint outline only at 1:20 (203 × 87 mm bookmark; 1:40 at 102 × 44 mm for the fob), 0.6 mm etched brass. Tiny, high-margin, zero shape ambiguity, no VW content whatsoever.  ·  FLOOR GRAPHIC FOR TRADE AND EVENTS at 1:1 on tarpaulin, rolled — the stand IS the bus, and staff serve from the +Y edge where the three bay gaps are.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** Every competing artefact in this category is a picture of a bus. This one is a picture of the ABSENCE of a bus, at full size, that you stand inside — and it is the only deliverable in the whole program whose value comes from the thing this project is genuinely world-class at and has never sold: measurement. It is also completely orthogonal to the trademark problem. A 1:1 plan of a converted vehicle's floor contact, with no elevation, no roundel, no two-tone livery and no recognisable profile, is not an image of a Volkswagen; it is a survey of a restaurant. And critically it is UNBLOCKED: it needs no lighting, no gloss, no colour management, no alpha channel, no large-format stitch, no 16-bit chain, and it does not touch the emblem — so it can ship while F191's hold on the delivery render still stands, without arguing with the hold at all. Contradicts nothing in F18.

**PRODUCTION.** Every figure on the floor comes from the machine and each one carries its own error bar, which is the point: STATE.md's measured-dimensions table reads wheelbase 2.4000 (+0.0 mm), track front 1.3690 (+0.0 mm), track rear 1.3590 (+0.0 mm), overall width 1.7497 (−0.3 mm), and the live verify block reads `dims L=4.065`. Body plan area is 4.065 × 1.750 = 7.11 m². Serving frontage is 3 × 0.5155 = 1.5465 m. The bay centres and widths are `BAY_CX = (0.6720, 0.0470, -0.5980)` and `BAY_W = 0.5155  # equal, measured` in t1_shell.py. DO NOT round the unequal pillars to make them match — the asymmetry is the evidence that this was measured off a real object. ONE TRAP, NAMED: STATE.md's headline row reads `overall length (ex counter) | 4.5800 | 4.0550 | +525.0 mm OUT` on a model that passes VERIFY — that 4.580 m is the PROPPED TAIL BOARD projecting aft, not the body. The floor rectangle is 4.065 m; the tail board's projection is drawn as the hatched break, not as the rectangle. Anyone who takes 4.58 off the authoritative file will lay a floor half a metre too long. The engraved type should be set in the brand's own condensed-caps civic voice — the same register the real bus carries hand-lettered on its counter fascia — and VACÍO should be the only word on the whole floor that is not a number.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** THERE IS NO PLAN CAMERA. `studio.views()` defines `topdown` as `lens=62` — a 62 mm PERSPECTIVE lens looking down from (2.60, 4.60, 6.40), so its verticals converge and it is a raised three-quarter, not a plan. A true orthographic plan view must be added (`plan`: ortho, straight down −Z, ortho_scale derived from build.py's own printed bbox with a stated margin). Small, contained, and it does not exist today. Note `topdown` has ZERO rendered-file mentions across ten revisions of ledgers, so nobody has looked at it either.  ·  A FOOTPRINT EXTRACTOR, and it should ask the MESH, not a render: project every body vertex to z = 0, take the hull, and emit it as a path. `trace_outline.py` already carries a `trace_with_holes()` whose header says its selftest checks the tracer on shapes whose area and perimeter are known BY CONSTRUCTION — reuse it rather than writing a new one, and add a row that compares the extracted rectangle's width and wheelbase against STATE.md's own published figures. That row is the whole quality guarantee of this artefact: if the floor is wrong, everything else about it is worthless.  ·  AN SVG/DXF EXPORT PATH. This is the binding constraint and it is bigger than this concept: `grep` for export_scene / gltf / usd_export / obj_export across the tree returns nothing, and the only persistence anywhere is build.py's `if os.environ.get("T1_SAVE"): bpy.ops.wm.save_as_mainfile(...)` — a switch that appears in ZERO handoff documents. A brass fabricator and a vinyl cutter both need vector. Until a path out exists, this concept is a drawing that cannot leave the building.  ·  TYRE CONTACT PATCHES need to be read off the mesh at z = 0 rather than typed — the tyres are now cut with a transverse tread (F308), so the contact patch is not a clean ellipse and should be drawn as whatever the geometry actually presents. Do not draw four identical ovals from the track figures alone; that would be a guard written against a pose.  ·  NOTHING ELSE. No lighting, no materials, no alpha, no post, no stitch, no emblem. This is the one concept in the set that is blocked only by a vector exporter.

**SCREENER.** 'An image of the bus that contains no image of the bus,' at 1:1, that you stand inside — and the VACÍO rectangle marked at the tail like a grave is a genuinely fine beat. The only concept unblocked by every project hold and gated solely on a vector exporter. Best due diligence in the batch: it alone caught that STATE.md's authoritative 4.5800 row is the propped tail board, not the body, which I confirmed in audit.py's _COUNTER_PARTS exclusion. Weakened by take-home SKUs (brass fob, tea towel) that are exactly the generic merch it otherwise avoids.

---

## THE CEILING — Stop selling the outside of the bus and start selling the inside of its roof — the painted lid, printed as an actual ceiling you sit underneath.  ·  screen 8.0  ·  `free3-1`

**THE IMAGE.** Not a picture of a vehicle. A flat overhead plate, composed as though you are lying on your back under the raised lid: the flower mural fills the entire field edge to edge, the olive-yellow menu strips run out past the trim on all four sides lettered in dark red, the bulb string crosses one corner in warm amber, and along the top edge a 60 mm band of blown-out, deliberately clipped daylight where the 1.110 × 2.034 m roof opening shows sky. That overexposed strip is the whole trick — it makes the printed field read as SHADE rather than as decoration, so the object performs as a canopy the moment it is over someone's head. PALETTE is the mural's own and nothing else: dark-brown ground, burnt orange, chrome yellow, dark red, olive-yellow strips. NO CREAM, NO BODY RED, NO VEHICLE. Second plate for the double-sided version: the same panel from underneath at a raking 30° so the lid's 22 mm edge, its bulb run and the leading corner of the counter enter bottom-frame, giving the ceiling an implied architecture. MOTION: a 6 s loop of the lid rising through 76° with the camera fixed under it, so the flowers swing down over the lens and the frame goes from sky to painted ceiling. Nothing else in the shot.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Shelter. Somebody painted the ceiling of the place where you are eating, and they painted it on the underside of a roof they cut off a van with an angle grinder. It is the feeling of a market awning, a parasol, a painted church ceiling, a hand over your head — and it is the exact emotional opposite of the entire category, which sells escape toward a horizon. The relationship between viewer and object is inverted: you are not looking at the bus, you are under it. It is also the only Tacombi artefact that works while you are already sitting down, which is when a restaurant customer actually has time to look at anything.

**FORMATS.** Stretched-fabric or printed-panel ceiling over the seating in the actual restaurants — the primary deliverable, and the one that is a permanent brand asset rather than a product.  ·  Canopy textile, 1200 × 1800 mm, single-sided, grommeted — sold, and also used over market stalls and pop-ups.  ·  Bandana, 550 × 550 mm, and a square silk scarf, 900 × 900 mm — both are the mural cropped to a square with the menu strips as the border, which is exactly how a scarf wants to be composed.  ·  Tin or pressed ceiling tile, 600 × 600 mm, for the domestic version.  ·  The INSIDE of the packaging lid — takeaway box, gift box, shipper. The customer opens the box and the ceiling is in there. Costs one extra print face.  ·  Album-format print, 300 × 300 mm, and a 700 × 700 mm framed edition.  ·  Phone lock screen and a 2560 × 1600 desktop — the only two 'ceilings' most people look at all day.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** There is no VW bus in it. There is no vehicle in it at all, no silhouette, no roundel, no trade dress, and therefore no trademark surface — which is the single cleanest legal posture available anywhere in this program. It is also the one graphic Tacombi owns outright and unambiguously: a mural painted by their people on their roof panel, held at 4096 × 2476 as `tex/lidmural.png`. And it exploits the object's actual best move, which the whole record already knows and has never merchandised — `t1_shell.py`: 'it swings up and over toward the show side, so its underside -- the flower mural with the yellow menu strips -- faces the counter. That is the one arrangement which reproduces all three photographs.' The competition sells a van going somewhere; this sells the roof it took off when it stopped.

**PRODUCTION.** EXPLICITLY NOT PAPEL PICADO. SPEC.md measures the band over the reference as 'a continuous flowering mass — 55.1 % foliage, 13.4 % crimson heads, 5.5 % cream florets … no flag silhouettes,' and the owner has parked the papel-picado question until the sticker is being built ('DO NOT RE-PUT IT UNTIL THEN'). This concept must be presented as the LID'S CEILING and must not drift toward bunting, or it will read as raising a question he closed. My own count of `tex/lidmural.png` resolves 68 distinct 32-step colour bins with 13 needed to cover 95 % of the field — that is a painting, not signwriting, so this is DTG / litho / dye-sublimation, NOT a screen-print separation. For the ceiling installation, print at 150–200 dpi at 1:1 from the 4096 master, which covers roughly 520–690 mm of width — so a full-size ceiling needs the artwork re-drawn or re-generated larger, not upscaled. Overhead installations are a fire-code and cleaning question in a working kitchen environment; specify a wipeable, flame-retardant substrate and get it signed off before anyone falls in love with a fabric.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** FIX F74(a) FIRST — IT IS THE WHOLE ARTWORK. The register, still OPEN at MEASURED-rev58: 'tex/lidmural.png is cropped at the bottom — the bottom row of flowers is sliced mid-bloom and the yellow side columns are truncated, where ref_side.jpg shows a board framed on all four sides. The render is faithful to the asset, so the defect is in lid_gen.py's output.' Printing this at a metre wide publishes the defect at a metre wide. And `lid_gen.py` is NOT called by `build.py`, so regeneration is a hand step or the render silently keeps the old file.  ·  CORRECT `lid_gen.py`'s HEADER IN THE SAME EDIT (rule 13). It says 'tex/lidmural.png   FRONT lid, OUTWARD (upper) face'. `t1_shell.py` says the mural is on the UNDERSIDE facing the counter and SPEC §4 says 'underside of the raised roof lid painted with flowers and menu strips'. Two live sources plus the machine outvote the header; leaving it contradicts the file that governs regeneration.  ·  THE LID'S OTHER FACE IS UNKNOWN AND UNBUILT. `build.py` runs `A(lid_skins, "paint")` then `A(lid_boards[0], "lidmural")`, so in the default build one face of the board is blank cream, and no frame we hold shows it. For a double-sided canopy that is a real gap and the honest answer is that it cannot be recovered from what we hold — either design the reverse deliberately as new artwork, or ship single-sided.  ·  Add a `lookup` camera under the lid at z ≈ 1.0, tgt z ≈ 3.0, wide (24–28 mm), to make the shade plate and the 76° rise loop. `LID_OPEN_DEG = float(os.environ.get("T1_LIDDEG", 76.0))` already parameterises the pose — but WATCH OUT: `LID_W = (LID_X0 - LID_X1) / LID_ASPECT / math.sin(math.radians(LID_OPEN_DEG))` couples the panel's WIDTH to its angle as 1/sin, so a naive sweep inflates the board instead of closing it. Freeze `LID_W` behind an ablation before animating.  ·  A SECOND CEILING MOTIF IS PHOTOGRAPHED, UNBUILT, AND IN NO CARRIER: the interior red scalloped valance with gold looped fringe visible through the windscreen in ref_playa_34.png, and the green sister ship's button-tufted ceiling with rosette medallions and a green scalloped fretwork valance in IMG_2073.jpeg. The second is artwork on a DIFFERENT vehicle and does not transfer under rule 11 — but it is design-language evidence that the lid interior is a canvas, and the valance on the red bus is genuinely ours and genuinely missing.
