Down the right margin, a single column of figures in the same 6 pt, ranged right, with nothing else: 0.7345 · 0.8250 · 0.6421 · 0.8384 · 0.8425 · 0.8528, and beneath a rule, the ceilings they were measured against: 0.9882 · 0.9465 · 0.9146. At the foot, one line, blind-embossed so it is felt before it is read: NINE TIMES. And in 8 pt below it, from the register: 'the ink is the right amount and the wrong arrangement.'

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Recognition, and it is not a comfortable one. Everyone who has ever tried to make something knows the specific state this sheet describes: you have measured the thing forty ways, you have built the instrument that measures the instrument, you have a number that says you are closer, and the person you are making it for looks at it and says 'Neither — both still wrong.' Nobody sells that feeling because nobody has the receipts for it. This project has 1,459 tracked files of receipts. The emotion is devotion made visible as error — and it rhymes exactly with the brand's own origin, which is a man buying a $3,000 bus that was missing three of its windows and cutting it open anyway.

**FORMATS.** Edition print, 700 × 1000 mm: litho cream ground, one screen-printed fluorescent-magenta hairline pass, blind-emboss chop, hand-numbered in pencil. Edition of 100–150. No signature — there is no hand to sign it; the chop authenticates it.  ·  BOOK: 'NINE TIMES', 96pp, 200 × 260 mm, section-sewn, plates from probe_scratch/ reproduced at their committed byte state with their own captions verbatim and nothing added. The wrong windows get the same page treatment as the right ones. Endpapers: the figures column, full bleed, 6 pt.  ·  A 2-minute silent film: 1,265 painted measurement PNGs at 12 fps, no music, no motion graphics, no logo, and one card at the end. Loops on the restaurant wall.  ·  Nolita restaurant wall, 2.4 × 3.4 m, printed at 3.5:1 of the sheet — the same file, so the hairlines become 1 mm rules and the 6 pt labels become readable at a metre.  ·  Small format that funds the rest: a 5 × 7 in postcard set of six single windows, each with its number on the back.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** Nobody in the VW-bus merchandise category has an archive of their own failure, because nobody else spent 76 revisions and 793 commits on a single vehicle. Every competitor's asset is a claim of success — a restored bus at golden hour. This is a claim of ongoing, documented, unresolved effort, and it is the only artefact in this entire program that could not be faked, bought, licensed or reproduced by anyone else on earth. It is also the exact inverse of the blueprint trope everybody keeps warning about: a blueprint is a draughtsman's fiction of a settled object, drawn after the fact; this is the mess of trying to know something and not getting there, with the failures left in.

**PRODUCTION.** THIS IS THE RISKY ONE AND HERE IS PRECISELY WHY, unhedged. (1) It puts a large, legible VW roundel at 187 mm on a saleable object — simultaneously the highest trademark-exposure element on the vehicle and the owner's single most-rejected object. Both the trademark posture and the fidelity ranking say do not do this. I am proposing it anyway, and it needs a lawyer on the actual artwork in the actual markets before a single sheet prints. (2) It requires the owner to be all right with his own frustration being the product; he could reasonably read it as mockery, and if he does, the answer is to withdraw it, not to argue. Put it to him with the sheet in front of him, as multiple choice, and let him kill it. (3) It is NOT the delivery render and must be labelled as a documentary artefact about the making — F191 and F193 gate the hero, not this. THE ESCAPE HATCH, and it is nearly as good: run the identical piece on the TAIL BOARD'S DARK ANGLED RECESS instead. F325 refused that feature on the measurement — one frame, a 30 × 12 px blob, 58 px below lum 90, n = 1 because the only corroborating frame is byte-identical (md5 f1b6f98c6a12b6e9ea0ec3edc68e945a) and the third is edge-on by construction. The caption writes itself and there is no trademark anywhere in it: 'IT CANNOT BE RECOVERED FROM WHAT WE HOLD.'

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** ALMOST NO MESH WORK — this is an archive-and-typography job. What it does need is genuine and non-negotiable.  ·  THE HAIRLINES MUST BE THE REAL WINDOWS. Write a script that dumps each probe's own selection polygon in image coordinates (probe_rev69_fitpose, probe_rev46_vw's C4/C6/C8, probe_rev69_emblem, probe_rev61 emblem --paint) and place THOSE, not redrawn approximations. A piece about a project whose entire ethic is 'paint the window before you publish the number' cannot ship with decorative windows. If a window cannot be dumped, it does not go on the sheet.  ·  FIX THE FRONT ORTHO CLIP BEFORE RENDERING THE GROUND. `views()['front']` is `ortho=3.55` with horizontal sensor fit; at 1600 × 1100 the vertical extent is 3.55 × 1100/1600 = 2.4406 m, centred on tgt z 1.52, i.e. z 0.2997 to 2.7403 — against a vehicle running from 0 to STATE.md's 'bbox top 3.132'. Both ends are cut. Derive ortho_scale from build.py's own printed bbox with a stated margin and add a row that asserts zero ink on all four borders, watched failing at 3.55 first.  ·  PULL probe_scratch/ AT ITS COMMITTED STATE, NOT AS IT SITS. F329: 17 probes repaint 42 tracked PNGs as a side effect, and rev 75 caught rev69_emblem_render.png going 24578 → 13500 bytes — smaller and degraded. `git checkout -- probe_scratch/` before harvesting anything, or the book reproduces a damaged plate of a painted window, which would be the most embarrassing possible failure for this particular piece.  ·  `T1_ALPHA=1` for the ground render, and do NOT route it through post.py — measured, post.py takes RGBA in and writes colortype 2, so the optics chain and the cut-out are mutually exclusive.  ·  VERIFY THE COUNT BEFORE PRINTING THE TITLE. 'Nine' comes from the register and the live brief ('THE EMBLEM IS STILL HIS NINTH REPORT'), but this project's own brief warns that such counts are hand-incremented and that the same finding carries four different revision counts in one file. Re-derive the nine from the ledgers' quoted owner turns and cite them by revision on the sheet, or the piece's headline is the one number on it that was never measured.

**SCREENER.** Aesthetically the most arresting sheet here and genuinely unfakeable, but the protagonist is the modelling process rather than the brand or the customer — it sells the maker's frustration back to a person eating a taco — and it puts a 187 mm roundel, the owner's ninth-reported defect, on a saleable object; the tail-board escape hatch ('IT CANNOT BE RECOVERED FROM WHAT WE HOLD', F325, n=1) is the better piece and should have been the proposal.

---

## The Other Side of the Hatch — Stop photographing the bus. Photograph FROM it — the cook's view out through the middle serving hatch, five seconds before service, with nobody there yet.  ·  screen 7.0  ·  `free4-0`

**THE IMAGE.** Camera INSIDE the galley, eye height ~1.55 m, standing where the cook stands behind the plancha, looking out through bay 2 — the middle aperture. FOREGROUND, near-black and thrown out of focus by a 90mm at f/2.8: the plancha's steel lip, the underside of the counter slab, a caddy, and the BACK of a pillar menu card (its printing reversed and invisible — you only ever see the blank side). MID-GROUND: the aperture's own cut edge, and the white bobble fringe running across the top of frame like a scalloped curtain rail. THROUGH THE HOLE: blown, overexposed daylight, deliberately clipped — no customer, no street, no scene, just a rounded rectangle of light, 515.5 x 403.0 mm at 55.0 mm corner radius (t1_shell.py: `BAY_W = 0.5155`, `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550`), i.e. a 1.279:1 canvas inside the canvas. PALETTE: warm steel, dark tan, cooking shadow; the only saturated colour anywhere is a sliver of flank red seen at grazing angle at the aperture's outer reveal, and the gold of the brass counter nosing catching light on the sill. LIGHT: single hard source from outside the vehicle, everything inside is bounce. COMPOSITION: square or 4:5 portrait, aperture placed low and slightly right so weight sits above it — the mural lid overhead never appears but is IMPLIED by warm orange reflected light on the ceiling of frame. A triptych extends it: one frame per bay. Bay 1 forward, bay 2 empty, bay 3 aft.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** You are on the labour side of the hole. Every VW bus picture ever made positions you as a tourist admiring a fun vehicle; this one puts you where the person who feeds you stands, before anyone arrives. What you feel is anticipation and a small privacy — the quiet before a shift. It converts 'I like this bus' into 'I know this place.' And it is warm without a single nostalgic signal: no sunset, no road, no wistfulness, just a hot griddle and a rectangle of daylight.

**FORMATS.** FREE PAPER TRAY LINER / PLACEMAT, 300 x 220 mm, duotone (warm black + brand red) on uncoated 60 gsm greaseproof. The reason this is the flagship format: the customer sitting at the counter is looking at the exact RECIPROCAL of the view they are currently in. Disposable, free, and the most keepable free object a restaurant can hand anyone. Duotone on absorbent uncoated is also the substrate that hides the render's two worst measured defects (see production notes).  ·  APRON, image printed INSIDE the bib so only the wearer sees it. Crew-only. Screen-print, one colour, 180 x 180 mm. Maximum insider payload at minimum broadcast.  ·  IN-RESTAURANT PRINT behind the counter, 900 x 900 mm, so the room physically contains its own inversion. Mounted flush, no frame — it should read as a second hatch in the wall.  ·  POSTCARD SET OF THREE (one per bay), 148 x 105 mm, uncoated 300 gsm, sold at the till for pocket money.  ·  INSTAGRAM CAROUSEL, three 1080 x 1350 frames — swipe moves you down the counter bay by bay. No copy on the images; the caption carries it.  ·  SHOP LOOP, 12 s, static camera, no cut: the only thing that changes is a hand entering the bottom of frame and the light shifting. Motion is the wrong verb here; stillness is the point.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** The entire VW-bus merchandise ocean is exterior three-quarter, because almost no VW bus has serving hatches and the few that do are never photographed from behind the counter. But the decisive argument is stronger than novelty: THIS IMAGE CANNOT BE PHOTOGRAPHED FROM THE REFERENCE SET. There are 15 reference images and only 10 distinct byte-sequences, and not one of them is an interior frame. It is the one picture that ONLY the 3D model can produce — which is the single best justification this project has ever had for seventy-six revisions of measurement. It also escapes the hippie misread entirely: the counterculture reading of this body shape lives outside on the flank, and there is no flank in this picture.

**PRODUCTION.** Duotone on uncoated absorbs both of the model's headline photograph-comparison failures, because a duotone carries neither a process red nor a specular: flank_compare on out/r75_side.png reads the render at median (196,81,67), G/R 0.422, against the photograph's (158,16,6), G/R 0.114 — it images salmon; and gloss_compare reads render spread 0.412 of the photograph's against a 0.60 bar, i.e. the paint reads matte. Neither defect exists in a two-ink interior. The blown aperture is deliberately CLIPPED — that is a legitimate photographic choice for a printed picture, but the clipped region must never be read back as a measurement (photometry.py's rule (b) refuses clipped data; that governs reading, not printing). Say so in the ledger so nobody later scores a gate off this frame. Do NOT route through post.py if any compositing is wanted: measured, RGBA in (colortype 6), colortype 2 out — post.py discards alpha and its docstring nowhere says so.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** AN INTERIOR CAMERA DOES NOT EXIST. studio.views() defines 14 cameras and the closest, `counter` (loc 3.40, 5.20, 1.98 / lens 90 / f6.3), is still OUTSIDE — and it has ZERO rendered-file mentions across LEDGER_rev66..rev75. Add a camera inside the galley volume at ~1.55 m looking +Y through a bay centre (BAY_CX = 0.6720 / 0.0470 / -0.5980).  ·  THE LIGHTING IS BUILT FOR THE OPPOSITE DIRECTION AND WILL POINT THREE LAMPS AT THE LENS. studio.py's galley fill is three per-bay boxes standing 0.48 m OFF the flank at y=1.35, spread 30 deg, watts 0.25 / 0.0 / 0.29, tuned so the bays read correctly FROM OUTSIDE against the photograph's own bay windows (render means 147.1 / 159.4 / 182.5 against ref_side.jpg's 147.7 / 158.0 / 178.3). An interior rig is required. NOTE: the source says 'BAY 2'S IS OFF' — which is exactly why bay 2 is the right hero frame, there is no fill lamp in the shot.  ·  F14 — `gal_end_f`'s sight lines — is the OLDEST INHERITED ROW IN THE REGISTER, seventeen revisions un-re-measured, and it is precisely the finding this image is about: what is visible past the galley's forward end. Re-measure it; this image is the reason to.  ·  F45 (roof-aperture interior) and F15 (A7, unlit roofed body, 6.92e5 px^2) are BOTH UNGATED and both sit directly behind this camera. Render one frame and LOOK before designing to it — the interior may come back dead black.  ·  The counter parts (`counter`, `counter_nosing`, `counter_top`) were authored to be seen from outside. Check the reverse faces exist and are not degenerate.  ·  For any CAB-interior variant: the white bobble fringe is under-built (STATE.md `fringe | 3`, the three serving apertures only) while the photographs show it round the WINDSCREEN too; and the interior RED SCALLOPED VALANCE WITH GOLD LOOPED FRINGE, plainly visible through the windscreen in ref_playa_34.png, is photographed, unbuilt, and in NO CARRIER anywhere in the repo. New geometry and a new register row.  ·  T1_ALPHA is not needed for this concept — it is a full-frame photograph, not a cut-out — which is why it can be shot before the thirteen-revision-old alpha path is revalidated.

**SCREENER.** 'This image cannot be photographed from the reference set' is the best justification 76 revisions has ever had, and the placemat as the reciprocal of the view the customer is sitting in is the smartest format in the brief — but behind-the-counter POV is now a common hospitality trope, and the whole thing rests on an interior that has never been lit or looked at (F15 and F45 both ungated), so budget for it rendering dead black.

---

## ÁBRELO — the pop-up — A card that opens into a taqueria: the lid rises, the counter cantilevers, the tail board drops, and a strip of gold foil along the drip rail catches the room light as the bulb string — the brand's founding gesture performed by the customer's own hands.  ·  screen 7.0  ·  `free5-2`

**THE IMAGE.** CLOSED: a 148 x 105 mm landscape card on 300 gsm cream uncoated, carrying the bus's TRUE side elevation die-cut flush to three edges. Three inks only — vermillion lower body, cream upper, and one gold hairline tracing the belt line's sweep down into the T1 V-swage. Nothing else on the front. It is deliberately a little dull; it looks shut. OPEN: the taqueria stands up. A 90° V-fold lifts the roof lid to its open angle with the flower mural facing the reader on the underside. A parallel fold pulls the counter out on its cantilever with the brass nosing along the lip. A slot-and-tab drops the tail board on its 38° diagonal. Three apertures punched through the flank — at the measured 515.5 mm bay width and the real 625/645 mm centre pitch, so the two pillars between them are 109.5 and 129.5 mm and deliberately NOT equal — reveal a printed inner leaf 8 mm behind: the galley in flat colour, racks and cans and the plancha and the warmer, with one multiply shadow. And along the drip rail, a 3 mm gold FOIL BLOCK that flashes as the card tilts — the bulb string, lit, with no light. Palette: five brand tokens and no more (vermillion, cream, folk-art gold, mural burnt-orange, dark maroon). Flat colour, one occlusion pass, no gradients, no photography. The paper does the rendering.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Delight, of the specific kind that only exists when the person's own hands cause the transformation. A film of the lid rising is something you watch; a card that opens is something you DO — and doing it is the difference between an advert and a toy. It is also the honest motion of this object: it has no engine, it does not drive, it OPENS. A child opens it twenty times in one sitting. An adult keeps it in a drawer for nine years. That is precisely the owner's own bar — 'should spark joy and be something families keep' — reached by a route he has not seen.

**FORMATS.** A6 pop-up card, 148 x 105 mm landscape, 300 gsm cream uncoated, 3 spot inks + one gold foil block, die-cut and creased. Given with the check or with a kids' meal. Flat-packs into a 155 x 112 mm sleeve so it ships and stores like a postcard.  ·  A5 press-out sheet — 'arma tu combi' — 350 gsm, die-cut, creased, tab-and-slot, no glue. Nets derived from TRUE orthographic elevations at a stated 1:32 (a 127 mm model), so it is dimensionally correct, which no papercraft VW on the internet is.  ·  594 x 420 mm concertina that opens to a ~1.2 m panorama of the bus deployed, for a shop window or a counter-back. Same mechanism, ten times the size, same three inks.  ·  A 2-second stop-motion of the card opening on a table, shot on a phone. The programme's only motion asset with zero render cost — and it is a better transformation film than a CGI one, because you can see a hand.  ·  Seasonal variant: the same mechanism with a different printed lid leaf. The lid is a canvas on the real vehicle, so a rotating lid is in character rather than invented.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** Papercraft VW buses exist and I will not pretend otherwise — and every one of them is A MODEL OF A VAN. This is A MECHANISM OF A CONVERSION: the three moving parts are the roof lid, the counter and the tail board, and Volkswagen made none of them. That is also the trademark position — the parts that move are the parts that are Tacombi's. It inverts the category's whole grammar: the ocean sells a flat picture of a bus in motion; this sells a three-dimensional object that arrives, stops, and unfolds, which is the brand's founding sentence performed by the customer. And it is the only artefact in this set where the model's real competitive advantage — being MEASURED — becomes something a person feels in their hands rather than something we assert in a caption. A pop-up whose folds are 3 mm out reads as cheap; one whose folds are right feels engineered, and nobody can say why.

**PRODUCTION.** CONTRADICTION WITH PRIOR ART, STATED PLAINLY: F18 locks a die-cut VINYL STICKER, style 'cartoon with rendered depth', scene 'nothing but the bus, die-cut tight, plus the sun and the papel picado', with the trigger 'build it after the model is done'. THIS IS NOT THAT ARTEFACT and is not offered as it — different substrate, different mechanism, no sun, and the papel picado is deliberately absent because the owner ruled 'Leave it open, I'll decide when the sticker is actually being built.' It shares F18's audience and emotional bar and nothing else, so it is not gated by F18's trigger; label it that way in the proposal or the hold will read as blocking it. CRAFT: pop-ups live or die on grain direction and crease depth — run the grain PARALLEL to the spine or the lid's V-fold cracks by the tenth opening. Prototype on the real stock at the real size before a line of finished art is drawn; a mechanism that works in 120 gsm mock-up dies in 300 gsm. Keep the gold as a FOIL BLOCK, never metallic ink — metallic ink on uncoated goes grey and the bulb string is the one element that must catch light. DO NOT print the folk art at card scale: measured on the shipped 4096² master, the flank gold's median stroke is ~13 mm on the vehicle, which at the card's ~1:31 is 0.42 mm — at or under a reliable positive line. Reduce to the three largest masses, which folk_gen.py's own census licenses: COMP_TOP = {1: 47.8, 2: 73.7, 3: 84.4, ...} and COMP_HIST's first three bins are 48 of 66 components carrying 2.29% of the ink. Delete the 48 and lose nothing.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** FIX THE CLIPPED ORTHOS BEFORE ANY NET IS DRAWN. views()['front'] and ['rear'] are ortho=3.55 with horizontal sensor fit, so at 1600x1100 the vertical extent is 3.55 * 1100/1600 = 2.441 m — against a vehicle that reaches z 0 at the tyres and, per STATE.md, 'bbox top 3.132' with the lids up. Both ends are cut. Derive ortho_scale from build.py's own printed bbox with a declared margin, and add a guard asserting zero ink on all four borders of any ortho frame — WATCH IT GO RED on the current 3.55 first.  ·  RENDER `rear` AND A PLAN. A paper net needs four elevations plus a plan; `rear` and `topdown` have no rendered file across ten revisions, and `topdown` is perspective anyway (see the plan-camera item in AQUÍ — the two concepts share it).  ·  DECOUPLE THE LID'S WIDTH FROM ITS ANGLE. t1_shell computes LID_W = (LID_X0 - LID_X1) / LID_ASPECT / math.sin(math.radians(LID_OPEN_DEG)) — the panel's WIDTH is tied to its opening angle as 1/sin, so sweeping the angle INFLATES the lid instead of closing it, and 0° divides by zero. The pop-up's whole geometry is a fold from shut to open, so it needs the shut and intermediate states to be real. Freeze LID_W at its shipped value behind an ablation switch and guard that the built panel's width does not move when the angle does.  ·  BUILD THE FLAT-COLOUR AND OCCLUSION PASSES — and restore the owner's own lost instruction while doing it. NEXT_CONTEXT_PROMPT_rev39.md §7 item 3 reads 'Style LOCKED by me: cartoon with rendered depth — vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset'; everything after the em-dash was dropped by rev 43 and has been in no carrier for 32 revisions. deliver.py already emits both halves per view (combi_%s_no_shadow.png and combi_%s_shadow_only.png). Put the clause back in a carrier and use it — it is exactly this card's pipeline.  ·  REPAIR tex/lidmural.png. F74a is open: the shipped master is cropped at the bottom, the bottom flower row sliced mid-bloom and the yellow side columns truncated, where ref_side.jpg shows the board framed on all four sides. The mural is this card's REVEAL — the thing the reader sees when the lid lifts — so it cannot ship cropped. lid_gen.py is NOT called by build.py, so regeneration is a hand step or the render silently keeps the old file.  ·  SAY OUT LOUD THAT THE GEOMETRY CANNOT LEAVE THE REPO. There is no export path — grep for export_scene/gltf/usd_export/fbx across the tree returns nothing, and the only persistence is build.py's undocumented T1_SAVE .blend, which appears in zero handoff documents. A paper engineer outside this project cannot be handed the model. For THIS concept they do not need it — dimensioned orthographic elevations are enough — but promise the elevations, not the asset.

**SCREENER.** The argument that the three moving parts are precisely the parts VW did not make is excellent thinking and the craft notes (grain parallel to spine, foil not metallic ink, drop 48 of 66 folk-art components because 13 mm becomes 0.42 mm at 1:31) are the work of someone who has actually made one — but pop-up and papercraft VW buses exist in quantity, so this wins on execution rather than idea and is the safest thing on the list.

---

## FILAMENT — Six seconds of near-black in which a single line of amber bulbs draws the bus out of nothing — and for ONE FRAME at the end you see what it actually is, in full colour, before it goes dark again.  ·  screen 6.5  ·  `t5-2`

**THE IMAGE.** FRAMING: `low34` — eye height 1.55 m, which is within 30 mm of the reference photograph's own derived camera height and which NO PREVIEW LIST HAS EVER RENDERED. Low, so the vehicle reads monumental rather than as a toy, and so the drip-rail line runs high and long across the frame.

LIGHT: none. The frame is BLACK — not dark, black. No ground, no horizon, no backdrop, no fill, no rim. The studio rig is off entirely and the emitters carry the entire picture.

WHAT IS VISIBLE: one continuous line of warm amber points and nothing else. The drip-rail festoon running the full length, the run that wraps the rear roof dome, and the line along the propped tail board's free edge. `BULB_PITCH = 0.0286` on a rail from `T._aft(-1.8000)` to `1.7000` — that is a dense bead, close to a continuous stroke, which is what the reference frames actually show and what the render currently fails to deliver.

PALETTE: TWO COLOURS. Warm amber and black. The vermillion, the cream, the gold and the silver exist in this film for 42 milliseconds.

MOTION — 6 s, and 95% of the runtime shows almost nothing:
1. Black. 12 frames. Long enough that the viewer wonders whether it is broken.
2. ONE BULB AT THE NOSE LIGHTS. Hold 8 frames. A single dot on a black field.
3. THE CHASE RUNS, nose to tail, ~14 bulbs a second, roughly 4 s to traverse. As it goes, the line DRAWS A SHAPE and you do not yet know what it is.
4. THE TURN. The line reaches the rear roof dome and CURVES OVER IT. That single turn is the whole piece: the moment the curve resolves, the silhouette lands and it is unmistakably a VW bus roofline. Then it runs back down the propped tail board at a different angle — the diagonal that says PROPPED OPEN, not parked.
5. Hold, all lit, 20 frames. The faintest possible bloom. Nothing else.
6. ONE FRAME of the whole vehicle, fully lit, in full colour, at rest.
7. Black. Loop.

COMPOSITION: the line is the only compositional element, so it must be drawn as a line — weight, taper, and the sag between fixings. The chase timing is NOT linear: fastest along the straight drip rail, and SLOWEST at the dome corner, so the moment of recognition gets the most frames of any beat in the film.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** RECOGNITION, DELAYED — and then a gift you half-doubt you saw. The entire loop is a withheld answer: you watch a line of light and your brain is actively trying to name the shape, and at the roof corner it lands. You get the specific pleasure of having WORKED IT OUT rather than having been shown, which is the only kind of brand recognition anybody enjoys. Then the single colour frame is the thing people rewind for — and rewinding is the only engagement metric that means anything. Underneath all of it is the most reliably warm image in the whole of hospitality: a shop turning its lights on at dusk. It never once shows you a product.

**FORMATS.** 6 s loop, 1080x1920 and 1080x1080. MP4 plus a 2-colour animated WebP that will land under ~200 KB, because there are two colours and most frames are black.  ·  WEB LOADING STATE / SPLASH. The chase IS a progress bar that happens to be a bus. It is the rare loading animation people would sit through.  ·  32 px animated favicon: three bulbs, chasing. It reads, because a moving point of light reads at any size.  ·  NEON OR ADDRESSABLE-LED SHOPFRONT SIGN — the roofline path bent in glass or run as an LED strip on the awning, doing the same chase. THIS IS THE FORMAT THE CONCEPT WAS BUILT FOR; the film is the pitch for the sign. Roughly 3-4 m across an awning; the path is a single unbroken run, which is what makes it fabricable.  ·  BACKLIT OOH — 6-sheet or transit-shelf panel, the amber path die-cut and lit from behind. In a dark station at night it is a lantern, not an ad.  ·  PRINT: the path alone, in a spot metallic or a spot UV gloss varnish, on uncoated BLACK stock at A1. The varnish catches the actual light in the actual room the poster hangs in; a printed highlight cannot, and an advert would never think of it.  ·  MERCH: the path as a single-ink chain-stitch or bean-stitch embroidery — a running stitch, so the 1.2 mm satin floor does not apply — 200x12 mm across a cap back, a jacket hem or a sleeve. One colour, one path, no VW mark, no body shape.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** THIS IS THE RISKY ONE AND HERE IS EXACTLY WHY A CAUTIOUS TEAM KILLS IT: you commissioned a photoreal 3D bus and for 5.9 of 6 seconds you cannot see it. No logo, no wordmark, no body colour, no product, no legible brand until a frame most viewers will consciously miss. Every instinct in a marketing review says 'show the bus'.

It is right anyway, for four reasons that are all measured. (1) THE LINE IS THE LOGO — it passes the logo-removal test perfectly, because the outline is recognisable with the word gone, and that is the property that makes a mark ownable. (2) IT SIDESTEPS EVERY OPEN DEFECT AT ONCE: the red images salmon (flank_compare live on out/r75_side.png: render median (196,81,67) G/R 0.422 against the photograph's (158,16,6) G/R 0.114) — not in frame; the paint reads matte (gloss_compare: spread 0.412 of the photograph against a 0.60 bar, FAIL) — not in frame; the emblem is the owner's NINTH report and F191/F234 both stand — not in frame; the tread's count is a declaration inside a measured 48..84 bracket — not in frame. (3) IT SIDESTEPS THE TRADEMARK EXPOSURE. There is no VW mark and, at one frame, arguably no trade dress. It is rare for the lowest-exposure option and the most distinctive option to be the same option. (4) IT SCALES FURTHER THAN ANYTHING ELSE IN THE PROGRAM — one continuous stroke works at a 32 px favicon and at 12 m of shopfront. And in a category built entirely on daylight, sunsets and beaches, absolutely nobody is doing the dark.

**PRODUCTION.** THE ONE-FRAME FLASH is the whole editorial risk and it must be handled twice: 1 frame at 24 fps in a controlled web player, 2 frames for social platforms that re-encode aggressively and will otherwise crush it into a visible cut or drop it entirely. Accept that the two versions differ and say so.

THE TURN IS THE FILM. Ignite fastest along the straight rail and slow markedly at the rear dome corner. If the recognition beat gets the same frames-per-bulb as the straight, the piece has no climax and is just a light chasing.

DO NOT FAKE THE GLOW IN POST. post.py's bloom is a global threshold pass (`BLOOM_THR = 0.94`); a painted-on halo over a dull sphere is exactly the defect F144 already names, and on a black frame with nothing else to look at it will be obvious. The emitters must genuinely clear the threshold in the render.

GRAIN WILL KILL THIS. post.py's `grain(..., seed=7)` is a FIXED SEED. On a near-black frame, identical grain every frame is a static dirt plate and will be the single most visible thing in the loop. Seed from the frame index or turn grain off entirely.

RENDER ECONOMICS ARE UNUSUALLY GOOD HERE: ~144 frames at 24 fps, but they differ only in which emitters are on, and a black frame with N emitters and no cyclorama, no bounce and no diffuse-heavy paint is far cheaper to sample than a full studio frame. Budget the single colour frame separately, at full delivery quality.

COMPOSE THE COLOUR FRAME OFF THE NOSE. That one frame is a normal delivery-quality still and inherits every open defect including the emblem. Use `hero34r` or `side`, never `hero34f` — and note it is a still pulled from a motion piece, not the delivery render, which remains under a hold the owner has placed four times (F191, F193).

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** BLOCKING: THE BULBS ARE ONE OBJECT WITH ONE MATERIAL AND CANNOT CHASE. t1_detail.py collapses them with `ob = join([ob, flex], "bulb_string")` and build.py binds a single `"bulb"` material; STATE.md lists `bulb_string | 1`. Keep the individual bulbs addressable (or drive emission from a per-instance attribute) behind an ablation so the joined path does not regress. This is the one change without which the concept does not exist.  ·  F144 MUST BE CLOSED: 'THE FESTOON BULBS READ AS PEARLS, NOT LIGHTS.' In a piece where the bulbs are the ONLY thing in frame, this stops being a nice-to-have and becomes the entire lookdev job. t1_mats has an `emissive(name, colour, strength=8.0)` builder and `T1_BULB_STR` / `T1_BULB_BASEV` are existing ablations. Push emission above post.py's `BLOOM_THR = 0.94`, render, crop it, and LOOK — this project's own first rule, and the defect it is meant to catch is precisely 'a thing that is supposed to glow and does not'.  ·  THE LID-FRAME BULB RUN IS NOT BUILT. The drip rail (`bulb_string`) and the tail board (`tb_bulbs`, `tb_bulbflex`) exist; the run along the raised lid's free edge that the photographs show does not. Beat 4's silhouette is significantly stronger with it. It is grounded in ref_workshop.jpg — but that is the GREEN vehicle, so it transfers as GEOMETRY, not as paint, under rule 11, and that distinction has to be stated when it is built.  ·  `low34` HAS NEVER BEEN RENDERED BY ANY PREVIEW LIST. Ten of studio.py's fourteen defined cameras are unexercised code. Render it and LOOK before designing to it — an unproven camera is not an asset.  ·  A BLACK-WORLD RIG. The studio is a white cyclorama and everything in the tree assumes it. This needs the key off, the world black, and the emitters carrying the frame. F259 licenses the move — 'Relight the studio and fix the ground if necessary' — with the single travelling constraint DO NOT change RED, which this concept honours by never showing it. Do NOT route it through `T1_SCENE=playa`: F57 records that path renders no vehicle at all.  ·  THE FESTOON PATH SHOULD BE EXPORTED AS A CURVE, not just rendered. The sign, the embroidery, the favicon and the print all want the line as vector geometry, and there is no export path of any kind in this repo — grep for export_scene / gltf / usd_export / fbx returns nothing, and the only persistence is build.py's undocumented `T1_SAVE`. A single-curve export is the smallest possible first export and would prove the route.  ·  Alpha: `T1_ALPHA` has not been run since rev 62 (no LEDGER contains the string) and every frame in out/ is colortype 2. This concept needs it less than the others — black is a real background, not a missing one — but the colour payoff frame wants it, and post.py destroys alpha anyway (measured: colortype 6 in, colortype 2 out).

**SCREENER.** Beautiful withheld-recognition structure and the turn at the roof dome is correctly identified as the whole film, but it is fatally casual about its own subject: F144 grades the festoon lever MEASURED-rev61 and EXHAUSTED with BOTH arms refuted by ablation — T1_BULB_STR=60 moved the ratio 1.40 to 0.88, WORSE — and the concept cites that same switch as an available fix; a chase-of-lights reveal is also a well-worn commercial device.

---

## The Warmest Thing in the Room — Nolita, February, indoors: a cold concrete market hall with one lit object in it, and the object is a bus that cannot drive.  ·  screen 6.5  ·  `t6-0`

**THE IMAGE.** Interior, Nolita market hall. Camera at roughly 1.05 m — a child's eye height, deliberately BELOW the 1.55 m that the reference photograph's own camera stands at (REF_MEASUREMENTS.md derives that height from the wall base and a man's shoe) — placed 4–5 m off the bus's front three-quarter on the serving side, 35–42 mm, f/2.8, focus on the near headlamp. THE ROOM IS COLD AND IT IS THE MAJORITY OF THE FRAME: painted cinder block, poured concrete floor (measured off ref_nolita_front34.jpg, window (560,400)-(660,450), median #5D6C70 — a genuinely blue-grey concrete), a produce stall's greens and yellows at the right edge desaturated almost to neutral, high fluorescents you never see. Push every neutral toward blue-grey and hold it there; nothing in the room is allowed a warm cast. THE BUS IS THE ONLY LIGHT SOURCE IN ITS OWN PATCH: both headlamps ON as warm discs (they are visibly lit in ref_nolita_front34.jpg and ref_nolita_front34b.jpg), the drip-rail festoon lit end to end, the three serving hatches glowing from the galley practicals, the chalkboard lid propped overhead like an awning with its own bulb run along the frame. A warm pool spills 2–3 m of floor in front of the bumper and dies at the edge of frame. FOREGROUND: the back of one small out-of-focus figure at shoulder height, STOPPED, not walking — no face, ever. Area budget is the whole design: the warm wedge (vermillion / cream / brass / ~2200 K bulb amber) occupies under a quarter of the frame and wins anyway. Motion arm, 6 s loop: room ambient breathes at a barely-there fluorescent flicker; the festoon IGNITES left to right over ~18 frames at 12 fps, stepped not tweened; then holds. Nothing else moves — no wheel, no drift, no push-in.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Shelter. The specific relief of finding a lit, warm, staffed thing inside a cold room in February — which is the actual experience of walking into Tacombi Nolita, and is the opposite of the escape fantasy the whole VW-bus category sells. The low camera is not a cute device: it puts the viewer at the height of the one person in ref_nolita_front34.jpg who has genuinely stopped to look, a small child in a magenta coat, and it makes a 4 m object read as a building you can walk into rather than a vehicle you could drive away in.

**FORMATS.** WINTER CAMPAIGN POSTER, A2 420 × 594 mm portrait at 200 dpi — reachable TODAY off the only large-format configuration this project has ever proven (LEDGER_rev57: 3840 × 2640, 256 spp, ten margined strips, worst seam z = 1.62). 3840 px is 488 mm at 200 dpi, so A2 fits with margin. Uncoated warm stock: the whole image is a cold field with a small warm hole, and a coated sheet's specular will fight the only bright thing in it.  ·  STOREFRONT WINDOW VINYL, 900 × 1400 mm — NOT available today and I will not pretend otherwise. At 200 dpi that wants ~7100 px on the long edge; nothing above 1600 × 1100 exists on disk (out/ is untracked and starts empty) and the stitch chain has never been exercised past 3840 (F49's seam guard exits 2 and F192 says 3840 is not the target). This is the format that legitimately triggers the owner's own open size question — F192's condition is literally 'ask it once he names the medium'.  ·  6-SECOND LOOP, 1080 × 1080 for social and 1920 × 1080 for in-store screens. Stepped at 12 fps, not 24: the render is not deterministic (measured floor ~2.04 % of pixels moving >8 levels whole-frame, 7.90 % on the body panels) and OpenImageDenoise here is spatial-only with no temporal pass, so a 24 fps tween will boil. A hand-drawn 12 fps cadence WANTS a live line — lean into it deliberately rather than discovering it in dailies.  ·  COUNTER GIVEAWAY POSTCARD, 148 × 105 mm, matte, printed one-up — the keepsake at the till. Stated plainly: this is a DIFFERENT ARTEFACT from F18's die-cut sticker. F18's style is locked to 'cartoon with rendered depth' and this is photoreal, so it must not be offered as, or against, that deliverable.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** Every image in the VW-bus merchandise ocean is OUTDOORS, in golden hour, and GOING SOMEWHERE — sunset silhouette, surf, palms, peace signs, 70s wash. This one is indoors, at night, on concrete, stationary, with the vehicle functioning as a LAMP rather than as a subject. There is no sky, no sunset, no palm, no road, no horizon. It also refuses the category's emotional stock outright: the trope sells escape, and Tacombi's own published account says the engine was scrapped and the transmission sold — this bus arrived once and stopped. The composition states that without a word, because the only thing it can do in the frame is glow.

**PRODUCTION.** F259 is the licence and it is an owner ruling, rev 71: 'Relight the studio and fix the ground if necessary' — the rig is open, the studio stays, and the one travelling constraint is DO NOT change RED (that is the option he declined). A cool market ambient is a relight, not a repaint. HONEST EXPOSURE, NAMED: this is the one of my three concepts that puts the VW roundel in frame, and that mark is the owner's NINTH report — probe_rev69_fitpose's P2 reads 0.8528 against P1b's own ceiling 0.9465, and P3 reads photo-only/model-only inside the ring at 73/178, 'NEAR BALANCED, so the ink AMOUNT is right and the ARRANGEMENT is wrong'. Mitigations, in order: the low three-quarter foreshortens the roundel and the headlamps out-read it; the Nolita chalkboard livery state is the correct dressing here and it is a plain-flank state with no folk art and no script (rule 11 — never mix that state's artwork with the Playa state's, which has already killed F99, F100 and F140); and this frame should ship LAST of the three. Do not route the frame through post.py if it is ever composited — post.py discards alpha (measured: colortype 6 in, colortype 2 out) and its live default is BACKDROP = 'headroom' at peak 252, not the white its own docstring claims, so a 252 field inside a #FFFFFF margin is a visible rectangle. Name the frame in every figure published off this.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** HEADLAMP EMISSION. The lamps must actually be ON — they are lit in both Nolita references and they are the compositional anchor. hl_lens is one of the five materials STATE.md still lists as CONSTANT roughness (amber, glass, lens, reflector, ruby) and I found no emitter bound to it. Needs an emissive lens plus a real IOR, and it must be watched failing (render with it off, crop, look) before it is believed.  ·  F144 IS OPEN AND IT IS THIS CONCEPT'S WHOLE SUBJECT: 'THE FESTOON BULBS READ AS PEARLS, NOT LIGHTS.' In ref_side.jpg the drip-rail string is a continuous warm bead, not a row of spheres. Emission must clear the bloom threshold so it flares. T1_BULB_STR and T1_BULB_BASEV already exist as ablations.  ·  A COOL INTERIOR RIG. Neither existing rig serves it: the studio is a featureless white cyclorama and playa() is explicitly solved as warm open shade with no sky and an absorbing canopy. Build a third — high broad cool source, concrete bounce, no sun. Licensed by F259.  ·  PER-BULB ADDRESSABILITY for the ignition. t1_detail currently collapses the string with join([ob, flex], 'bulb_string') and STATE.md lists it as one object with one material, so a chase is not drivable. Keep the bulbs separate behind an ablation so nothing regresses.  ·  T1_ALPHA EXERCISED — it has not been run since rev 62 (no LEDGER contains the string), every frame in out/ is colortype 2, and deliver.py correctly refuses them ('4 checked, 1 FAILED -- C1', 'PACKAGE IS NOT TRUSTWORTHY'). Needed if the room is composited rather than built.  ·  OPTIONAL BUT CHEAP AND VISIBLE: tex/senor.png has no dark ink at all (of pixels with alpha>200 the fraction below luma 80 measures 0.0000) against SPEC §3's requirement of 'SILVER with a dark keyline and drop shadow'. Not needed for the Nolita plain-flank state — noted here because it blocks the other two.

**SCREENER.** Emotionally the truest reading in the set — shelter in February, camera at the height of the one child who actually stopped — with real observed detail (concrete measured at #5D6C70), but 'warm lit thing in a cold room' is the oldest hospitality photograph there is, so this is beautifully executed familiarity rather than strangeness, and it is the only concept that puts the owner's ninth-report roundel in frame.

---

## TRES AUSENCIAS — The Three Absences — A triptych of photographic plates in which the subject of each image is a thing the bus does not have: no engine, no transmission, no windows — the three subtractions that turned a $3,000 van into a restaurant.  ·  screen 6.5  ·  `free0-0`

**THE IMAGE.** Three plates, same treatment, hung as a set, portrait, always in this order.

PLATE I — SIN MOTOR. Camera in the driver's seat, 30 mm equivalent, eye at 1.15 m, looking AFT down the barrel of the vehicle. Near field, hard-silhouetted and almost black: the steering-wheel rim cutting the bottom-left third, the gear lever's knob, the top arc of the blank speedometer bezel. Mid ground: the galley — steel worktop, the plancha, racks, hooks, bottles — lit only by what falls through the openings. Far field: the three serving hatches blown to 95% white, so the image runs black-to-white front-to-back along one axis. The driver's position now faces a kitchen. Palette: near-black warm neutrals, one steel grey, and the three white rectangles. Almost no red at all — the red is behind the camera.

PLATE II — SIN TRANSMISIÓN. Straight-on, orthographic, tight: the engine lid propped open at the tail, and behind it a lined compartment that is EMPTY. Not staged empty — modelled empty. Flat frontal light, the cream lid edge a hard bright line at the top of the frame, then a rectangle of near-black with a 2.8 mm returned edge catching one specular. The whole plate is about 60% dark void. Type below the image, 8 pt, Spanish: *el motor fue desechado, la transmisión vendida.*

PLATE III — SIN VIDRIOS. The three serving apertures, orthographic, dead-on, filling the sheet edge to edge — 515.5 × 403.0 mm apertures on 625/645 mm centres, so the two pillars between them are 109.5 and 129.5 mm and the rhythm is deliberately UNEQUAL. White bobble fringe scalloping the inner edge of each. Galley visible through them, soft. Nothing else in frame — no wheels, no roof, no nose, no emblem. Reads as a facade, or as three doors.

Set type: one hairline rule under each image; caption in Spanish first, English half-size beneath; no wordmark on the image, wordmark blind-embossed on the mount only.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Tenderness toward a machine that gave up what it was for. Plate I is the one that lands: you are sitting where the driver sat, holding a wheel that steers nothing, looking at a working kitchen where the road used to be. It is a retirement portrait. Nobody feels nostalgia here — nostalgia is about a thing that is gone, and this thing is open for lunch. The feeling is closer to respect: it stopped so that this could happen. And the negative framing does the emotional work for free — an absence is something a viewer completes themselves, which is why it sticks.

**FORMATS.** THREE 500 × 700 mm PLATES, spot-colour litho or photogravure-look duotone on 300 gsm uncoated warm white. Uncoated because these images are 60% dark and matte: a coated stock would put a glare on the void and the void is the subject. Sold and hung as a set of three only — split them and the argument collapses.  ·  IN-RESTAURANT INSTALL: the same three at 700 × 1000 mm behind the counter of every location. This is the piece that goes on OUR wall, not the customer's — it tells staff and regulars what the company thinks it is.  ·  POSTCARD SET OF THREE, 105 × 148 mm, given with the bill, unbranded on the face, one Spanish line on the reverse. Cheapest possible way to put a genuinely strange image in a stranger's coat pocket.  ·  PACKAGING: the three plates as the ONLY art on takeout boxes and the paper bag — one plate per SKU, rotated. Printed small (~90 mm) and in one dark ink, so they read as marks rather than photographs.  ·  EDITORIAL / ANNUAL: a four-page insert, three plates plus a fold-out of the founding paragraph set as a poem. Register: quiet, no exclamation, no superlative.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** The entire VW-bus merch category sells possession and departure — what the bus HAS (surfboards, roof rack, sunset ahead) and where it is GOING. This is a set of three pictures of what the bus is missing, and it can only be made by the party that removed those things. It also physically cannot be shot: the interior of the actual Playa bus is a working kitchen at service temperature with staff in it, and no photograph in the reference set looks aft from the driver's seat — so this image exists nowhere on earth except as a render. Additionally, none of the three plates contains the VW roundel, the front bumper, or the nose — the three most trademarked and most generic surfaces on the object are out of frame in all three by construction, which is a trade-dress posture and a distinctiveness strategy at the same time. Contradicts nothing in F18; this is explicitly not the sticker and not the delivery render.

**PRODUCTION.** Grounding, quoted: build.py's own comment on the tail compartment reads — "Nothing is invented to fill the bay: PHOTOS_WANTED_rev44 records that 'the engine was scrapped and the transmission sold', so its contents are unknown" and "where a T1's engine bay is a dark cavity". The emptiness in Plate II is a deliberate authoring decision already in the source, not a set dressing. SPEC.md §0.1 grades the founding account S (source): the bus was green, cost $3,000, was "missing three of its windows" and "only had the front bench". Plate III's geometry is measured, not styled: t1_shell.py carries `BAY_W = 0.5155  # equal, measured` and `BAY_CX = (0.6720, 0.0470, -0.5980)  # measured centres`, with `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550`, and STATE.md's live block reads `open serving apertures on +Y: 3` / `bay widths 0.516 0.515 0.516`. The unequal pillars (109.5 / 129.5 mm) are the detail that makes it read as observed rather than drawn — do not regularise them. Plate I's blank speedometer is real: t1_detail.py builds `speedo_face` as a plain disc on the `dark` material with no dial, no numerals and no needle — a speedometer with nothing on it, in a vehicle with no engine. That is the single best small detail in the set; put it in frame. CEILING TO CARRY: I have not rendered any of this and out/ is untracked, so Plate I is a composition proposal, not an observed image. Ship the three at one exposure and one grade or the set breaks.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** THE AFT SIGHTLINE FROM THE DRIVER'S SEAT HAS NEVER BEEN RENDERED OR MEASURED, and the register knows it: F14 (gal_end_f's sight lines) is the OLDEST INHERITED row in OPEN_FINDINGS, un-re-measured for seventeen-plus revisions, and it is exactly about what you can see through the galley. Plate I is blocked on it. First move: add a `cab_aft` camera and render one frame at preview size and LOOK — do not assume the barrel is clear. There may be a bulkhead (`galley_backdrop`, `gal_backdrop_f`) closing the shot entirely, in which case Plate I becomes a shot of a wall and the concept needs re-cutting.  ·  INTERIOR LIGHTING DOES NOT EXIST FOR THIS. The rig is a white cyclorama for exteriors. Plate I's whole composition depends on the galley being lit ONLY by light falling through the three apertures — that is a new lighting setup, and F45 (roof-aperture interior) and F15 (unlit roofed body, 6.92e5 px²) are both ungated, unmeasured items sitting directly behind this frame. Owner ruling F259 licenses exactly this work: "Relight the studio and fix the ground if necessary" — the rig is open, RED is not.  ·  PLATE II NEEDS THE ENGINE LID OPEN AND THE BAY LOOKED AT. `lid_trunk` and `trunk_bay` are built and `TRUNK_OPEN_DEG` is a hardcoded 0.0 — add `T1_TRUNK_OPEN`, render one tail frame, crop it and look. The register records that this exact object once shipped with NO MATERIAL and rendered as the brightest thing on the tail; check it renders as a cavity before believing the plate.  ·  PLATE III wants a dedicated orthographic aperture camera framed on the band Z_SILL..Z_HEAD, not a crop of the 5.90 side ortho — a crop loses resolution exactly where the bobble fringe lives.  ·  NOT BLOCKED BY, BUT MUST BE STATED AGAINST: F191 and F193 hold the delivery render. These are three exploratory plates for an in-restaurant install, not the held frame. Say so in the proposal or the hold reads as blocking work it does not cover.

**SCREENER.** Photographing the three subtractions is a good inversion and Plate I — sitting where the driver sat, holding a wheel that steers nothing, facing a kitchen — is a real retirement portrait, with the blank speedo_face disc as a superb corroborating detail. Fatal to the SET as specified: Plate II wants the engine lid propped open and proposes adding T1_TRUNK_OPEN, but t1_shell.py carries TRUNK_OPEN_DEG = 0.0 under '*** SHUT, BY THE OWNER'S RULING AT REV 49 ***' — the concept never mentions it, in a set it insists collapses if split. Absence triptychs are also a familiar gallery gesture.

---

## THE ROOM THAT USED TO BE A VEHICLE — Render the inside — which nobody has ever done — and print it at 1:1 as architecture, under one rule: the bus is never reproduced smaller than life.  ·  screen 6.5  ·  `free2-1`

**THE IMAGE.** Two images and one rule. IMAGE A, THE STANDING SHOT: camera inside the body at eye height ≈ 1.55 m, which puts the lens level with the aperture heads (Z_HEAD 1.710 AG) and the head inside the roof aperture — STATE.md records the crown cut away at mid-wheelbase, 'no roof above 1.710 m at |y|<0.30'. Looking aft along the galley: the steel worktop at GAL_Z_WORK 1.5010 running away from you, the backdrop plane at GAL_Y_BACK −0.480, the appliance base topping out at GAL_Z_TOP 1.4285. On your left, three rectangles of hot daylight punched clean through the wall — 515.5 × 403.0 mm each, 55 mm corner radius, sill at 1.307 AG — with the white bobble fringe scalloping their inner edges and the world outside blown to nothing. Overhead: the mural lid propped open against sky, a painted ceiling three metres up on two struts. Everything else in warm shadow. The palette is one this project has never used and it is sitting measured in t1_detail: GAL_WHITE (0.7300,0.7300,0.7355) neutral aft of a material seam the record actually measured at GAL_SEAM_X −0.6140, GAL_CREAM (0.7500,0.7040,0.6420) warm forward of it, plus GAL_STEEL, GAL_GREEN (0.1450,0.3050,0.1750), GAL_AMBER, GAL_RED, GAL_DARK. Light: three hard shafts through the hatches, one soft wash from the roof opening, the plancha and the bulb string as practicals. IMAGE B, THE DEVELOPMENT: the interior's surfaces unrolled flat into a single continuous 4.065 m strip — an architect's development drawing, not a render. Back wall, both ends, the show side with its three holes, drawn as measured elevation with the galley in section, the counter's 321 mm plan depth (CNT_Y_IN 0.845 → CNT_Y_OUT 1.166) shown as a shelf, hairline dimension rules, no perspective anywhere. THE RULE, which is the actual idea: nothing in this program is ever reproduced smaller than life. The vehicle exists in the world only as full-size fragments — a 4.065 m wall, a 515 mm hatch, a 274 mm hubcap, a 1963 plate — and the whole bus only ever assembles in a room you walk through.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** You are behind the counter, and it is smaller than you thought. Every image ever made of this object — and of every VW bus ever sold on a t-shirt — is of the OUTSIDE, from far enough away to admire it. Step inside and the fantasy inverts: this is a galley kitchen with three holes in the wall, hot, cramped, competent, and somebody's job. The feeling is not wanderlust, it is belonging — the particular pride of being on the working side of a counter. And the 1:1 rule does something no poster can: it refuses to let you hold the bus. You can't put it on a shelf, you can't shrink it to fit your wall. You have to stand next to it at the size it actually is, which is the only honest relationship you can have with an object whose whole story is that it stopped somewhere and became a place.

**FORMATS.** WALLCOVERING, panels totalling 4065 mm × ~1500 mm, restaurant interiors — the unrolled bus interior at 1:1, so a service corridor or a queue wall becomes the inside of the founding vehicle.  ·  STOREFRONT WINDOW VINYL: three 515 × 403 mm apertures, 55 mm radius, sill line at exactly 1.307 m off the pavement, so people on Elizabeth Street look through 'the bus' into the actual restaurant. Cost: near zero. Payoff: the building becomes the vehicle.  ·  1:1 hubcap as a physical object — 274 mm is a dinner plate, a wall clock, a tray.  ·  1963 door plate at true size, chrome frame, empty.  ·  The development drawing as a large-format print, 1189 × 841 mm (A0), which is the one item allowed to be under life size because a drawing is a drawing.  ·  A 4-panel folding screen / room divider at 1:1 for pop-ups.  ·  Interior-panorama phone wallpaper and a slow 8-second pan as the only motion piece — no hinges, no driving, just the light moving across the worktop.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** The entire category sells the exterior silhouette, shrunk to fit a garment or a sheet. Not one competitor asset shows the inside of the vehicle, because not one competitor has the inside modelled — and this project does: 229 mesh objects including roughly thirty gal_* meshes, plancha, galley_top, galley_backdrop, a full cab with dash, speedo, pedals, seats and door cards. It is the single largest unexploited asset in the repository, and I verified nobody has ever pointed a camera at it: all 14 declared views sit outside the body footprint, none inside. The 1:1 rule is the second escape, and it is structural rather than stylistic — a jaded art director has seen every treatment, but 'we never print this object smaller than it is' is a constraint, and constraints are what make a program look authored rather than assembled.

**PRODUCTION.** Honest warning: this is the most expensive of my three and it is gated on an unknown. F45's roof-aperture half is still OPEN, UNGATED and never measured — 7.4 × 10⁵ px² of a 3840 delivery frame with no instrument on it — so the interior may render badly and nobody knows. What IS measured cuts the right way: F98 refuted the old 'untextured white blocks' wording and found the interior/exterior cream value ratio at render 0.705 against the photograph's 0.774, i.e. the render's interior is DARKER than the photograph's, not blown out — which is good news for the standing shot and bad news for anyone hoping the galley reads without a lighting pass. Take Image B off the drawing board, not the render farm: 4.065 m at 150 dpi is ~24,000 px, and nothing above 4320 × 2880 has ever been rendered in this project's history while the current chain is proven only once, at 3840 × 2640. So the development is vector line plus a sampled occlusion pass — which is exactly the owner's own lost style clause, recovered from NEXT_CONTEXT_PROMPT_rev39 and missing from every carrier since rev 43: 'vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset'. deliver.py already emits its two inputs as combi_*_no_shadow.png and combi_*_shadow_only.png. Also: everything downstream of the render is 8-bit (stitch.py and post.py both read through PIL on 16-bit files), which is a banding risk on a large flat wall and must be fixed or dithered before any 1:1 job.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** ADD INTERIOR CAMERAS. There are none. Two at minimum: standing at eye height mid-body, and low behind the counter looking out through a hatch.  ·  MEASURE F45 BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE. The roof-aperture interior is ungated and unmeasured; F15 (A7, unlit roofed body, 6.92e5 px²) is in the same state. This concept lives or dies there and both findings are currently sitting open with no instrument.  ·  INSPECT EVERY INTERIOR SURFACE FROM WITHIN — doorcard ×2, doorback ×2, cab_floor, van_floor, galley_backdrop, wheelhouse ×4. Backfaces, normals and material assignment have never been looked at from inside, and this project's own first rule is that every defect it has shipped passed VERIFY and was found by looking at a crop.  ·  RESOLVE WHICH FACE OF THE ROOF LID CARRIES THE MURAL — it is the ceiling of this concept and the tree contradicts itself: lid_gen.py says 'FRONT lid, OUTWARD (upper) face', SPEC §4 says 'underside of the raised roof lid'. In the default build the other face is plain cream (build.py: A(lid_skins, "paint") then A(lid_boards[0], "lidmural")), and no frame we hold shows the reverse. This may be a genuine 'cannot be recovered from what we hold' and therefore an owner question with a crop attached.  ·  REGENERATE tex/lidmural.png. F74(a) is open — the shipped master is cropped at the bottom, flowers sliced mid-bloom and the yellow side columns truncated, where ref_side.jpg shows the board framed on all four sides. And in out/r75_hero34r.png it renders as a dense repeating rosette tile rather than the photograph's stemmed flowers on a dark ground. Overhead at 1:1 that is unmissable. lid_gen.py is NOT called by build.py — regenerate by hand.  ·  GROUND OR CROP THE BOBBLE FRINGE. It scallops the inner edge of every hatch in these compositions, and t1_detail says plainly 'Ball diameter and pitch are INFERRED (each ball is ~2 px)'.  ·  F72 is open and sits in frame: the exterior counter props read within 2.4 DN of the painted body where the photograph shows a 60+ DN spread, and gal_caddy 'has no dark frame member at all'.  ·  RUN T1_ALPHA ONCE — unexercised for thirteen revisions, every frame in out/ is colortype 2 — and note post.py destroys alpha, so the optics chain and any cut-out are mutually exclusive.  ·  Add a 16-bit read path to stitch.py and post.py, or a deliberate dither, before any 1:1 wall job.

**SCREENER.** 'Never reproduced smaller than life' is a genuinely strong programmatic constraint, and recovering the owner's lost rev-39 style clause (vector line, occlusion sampled from the asset) is excellent archaeology; surfacing that lid_gen.py and SPEC §4 disagree about which lid face carries the mural is a real finding. But it is three concepts stapled together, the most expensive of all ten, gated on F45 which is open and has never been measured, and it duplicates the interior of free1-0 and the 1:1 of free1-1 without beating either.

---

## Relic — Six worn things at near life size, shot as still lifes on black — the hubcap, the empty 1963 plate, the tarnished word, the arch lip, the brass nosing, one lit bulb — and the bus is never shown whole.  ·  screen 6.0  ·  `t0-1`

**THE IMAGE.** SIX PRINTS, ONE SET. Each is a single object floating in dead black: no ground line, no horizon, no context, no bus. Scale is 1:1 or near it, so the print is the size of the thing.

LIGHT, identical across all six so they read as one set: a single low raking key from the left at about 15 degrees above the surface, so every scuff and every pit throws its own micro-shadow and the relief does the work; a weak cool fill at maybe 1/8 so the shadow side stays readable without going grey. Shallow depth — f/2.8-equivalent — focused on the NEAR EDGE of the mark, everything behind falling soft. The rig already reports its own DoF numerically (aim() logs near/far/hyperfocal and sets 9 aperture blades with an 11-degree rotation, so speculars go nine-sided rather than perfect discs). Use that.

PALETTE: each frame carries only its own object's colours against black. That constraint is what makes six different subjects read as one family.

THE SIX:
1. THE HUBCAP — 274 mm (CAP_D = 2 x (0.1345 + 0.0025)): black tyre shoulder, cream rim dish, red dome, cream VW badge at 86.9 mm. With the kerb scuff and the radial scratch that ref_side.jpg shows across the dome, and the brown grime settled in the lower rim dish. Four concentric bands of the brand palette, damaged.
2. THE PLATE FRAME — 330 mm (PLATE_W) of dull, pitted chrome, aperture EMPTY, red bodywork showing through it, '1963' embossed on the upper rail. Cropped so it reads as a picture frame around nothing.
3. THE WORD 'SEÑOR' — that word alone, tarnished, at about 2:1. Warm oxide blooming across silver leaf. This is the most beautiful image in the set and it is straight off ref_side.jpg.
4. THE ARCH LIP — the rear wheel-arch edge where the paint has gone, tyre shoulder below, gold acanthus running up out of frame above. Long thin format.
5. THE COUNTER NOSING — 40 mm of brass, burnished bright along its working length, dull and dark at the ends nobody touches. An extreme long-thin print, 1200 x 200 mm.
6. THE BULB — one festoon lamp on its brass rail, lit, at 3:1. The only warm light source in the whole set, and the only thing in the six that is switched on.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** The tenderness you feel for a scratch on something you own. A fragment moves people more than a whole object because the viewer has to assemble the rest, and assembling it is an act of affection.

There is also a quiet, generous joke running through the set: it is a bus brand's merchandise where the bus never appears. Someone who ate at that counter recognises the hubcap and the brass nosing immediately and is inside the story. Someone who didn't just owns six good photographs. That asymmetry — rewarding the insider without punishing the stranger — is exactly what makes an object get kept rather than worn once.

And there is no advertising in it at all. There is no logo to remove, because there is nothing to remove it from.

**FORMATS.** Set of six, boxed: 300 mm square (hubcap), A3 landscape (plate frame), 800 x 300 mm (the word), 900 x 300 mm (arch lip), 1200 x 200 mm (nosing), 300 mm square (bulb). Matte or soft-textured 300 gsm uncoated — the blacks have to stay deep and a gloss stock will mirror the room straight back at the viewer.  ·  Sold singly as well as boxed. The set is the upsell; the single is the entry price. Print the set number and the plate number on the reverse, not the face.  ·  Apparel, one relic at LIFE SIZE: the hubcap at 274 mm is a near-perfect chest print, and at 1:1 every stroke on the garment is the same width it is on the vehicle, so nothing is reduced and nothing collapses. The counter nosing at 1200 x 40 mm runs a sleeve or a hem.  ·  Bandana / scarf, 550 mm square, the six tiled on black. Silk-feel or heavy cotton — a bandana is a working object, which is the right register.  ·  Postcard set, 105 x 148 mm, six cards. Free at the counter, or in the bag with an order. This is the version that reaches most people.  ·  Restaurant banners: the ARCH LIP and the NOSING as long-thin panels above the pass. They are already the right proportion for a strip.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** The entire VW-bus merchandise market shows the whole bus, without exception. Sunset silhouette, surf, hippie/flower-power, 70s wash, minimal line art, blueprint, pop-art colourway — every one of those tropes REQUIRES the silhouette, because the silhouette is the thing being sold. A set that never shows it cannot be mistaken for any of them, at any distance, by anybody.

It also removes the two exposed elements entirely: the trademarked bus SHAPE never appears, and the VW roundel appears only as an 86.9 mm hubcap badge which can be cropped out or replaced. The commercially safest set on the table is also the most distinctive one, which is the tell that it is the right idea rather than a compromise.

And it is the only concept here that gets BETTER as the model improves, because it lives entirely on material — chrome pitting, brass burnish, chalked leaf, rubber bloom. Every other treatment is bounded by composition; this one is bounded by lookdev, and lookdev is what a photoreal asset is actually for.

**PRODUCTION.** THESE ARE NOT CROPS OF A WIDE FRAME, AND THAT IS THE COUNTERINTUITIVE PART. At the 734 px/m a 3840 px frame gives, the 274 mm hubcap is 201 px — unprintable. A 1:1, 300 dpi hubcap needs ~3200 px across 274 mm, which is a normal 3200 px render of a 0.30 m subject. So the relic series is CHEAPER in render terms than the elevation, not more expensive, and it is the only thing this chain can honestly print large today. The camera class already exists: `detail_f` at 100 mm, f/6.3.

RENDER EACH RELIC AS ITS OWN FRAME, name it, crop it and LOOK at it before it goes anywhere. This project's own list of defects that passed VERIFY and were caught only by looking includes 'a disc of body red at the centre of every tail lamp' and 'hubcaps that render as five-petal flowers' — both of which are IN THIS SET. I looked at ref_rear34.jpg at 2x and confirmed the tail lamp is a small AMBER-BROWN oval in a thin bright bezel, not red.

DO NOT DRAW WEAR FROM THE MODEL — the model has none. Draw the hubcap scuff and the arch-lip loss from ref_side.jpg, where they are visible, and author them. Inventing damage is the one thing this territory cannot survive, because the entire claim is that these are THIS vehicle's marks.

CARRY ONE CEILING ON RELIC 1: STATE.md says in terms 'hubcap badge is SELF-CONSISTENCY ONLY -- CAP_EMBLEM_WFRAC has never been compared to a frame; see PHOTOS_WANTED item 7.' A 1:1 hubcap print publishes an unverified proportion at maximum size, which is exactly the wrong direction. Either close item 7 (one square-on close hubcap photograph — a five-minute ask) or crop the badge and print the rim geometry, which IS measured.

BLACK GROUNDS AT 8 BITS WILL BAND. Same PIL truncation as everywhere else; a soft falloff from a raking key into black is the worst case for it. Route through photometry.read_png and keep 16 bits, or dither on export.

AND DO NOT ROUTE THROUGH post.py IF ANY RELIC NEEDS A CUT-OUT — measured, it reads rgba[..., :3] and saves 3 channels, so optics and alpha are mutually exclusive and its docstring never says so.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** THE RED CARRIES NO EDGE CHIPPING AT ALL, SO RELIC 4 CANNOT BE RENDERED TODAY. t1_mats.py says it in its own comment, measured and painted: 'as shipped the whole red shell is BLACK -- no edge signal at any arch lip, panel aperture or shut line', with 0.000000 of the rear arch lip clearing W_EDGE_LO against 0.208417 on the true normal. AND BOTH OBVIOUS FIXES ARE ALREADY REFUTED IN THAT SAME SOURCE: Pointiness is per-vertex and mesh-density-dependent, and `True Normal` was tried and WITHDRAWN because on a smooth-shaded mesh it 'detects TESSELLATION, not folds'. The route that is not refuted is to stop deriving wear procedurally and AUTHOR it — a painted chip/wear mask in the same texture pipeline as swirl.png, registered to where the photographs actually show damage. That is also the territory's whole thesis: wear is authored history, not a noise field.  ·  FIVE MATERIALS ARE STILL CONSTANT ROUGHNESS — amber, glass, lens, reflector, ruby (STATE.md). Relic 6 (the bulb) and any tail-lamp variant both need `lens` to stop being constant, or the glass renders as a plastic button. In my crop of out/r75_hero34r.png the headlamps already read as chrome buttons.  ·  CHROME NEEDS TARNISH AND PITTING VARIATION. In ref_rear34.jpg at 2x the 1963 plate frame is dull, mottled and pitted; the render's brightwork is uniform. Separately SPEC §3 grades it R that 'headlamp bezels read warm/brass, not bright chrome' — and ref_playa_34.png agrees — against the render's cold chrome. Ground it on a painted window with photometry before touching the material.  ·  THE BRASS NOSING NEEDS A WEAR GRADIENT ALONG ITS LENGTH — bright where plates and forearms cross it, dull at the ends. It has no parametric handle for that today, which is relic 5's entire subject.  ·  T1_SENOR_TARNISH=1 FOR RELIC 3. It restores the measured tarnish byte-for-byte (TARNISH_K = (0.675, 0.374, 0.276), a measured 'Señor'-median / clean-silver-median ratio). SAY PLAINLY WHAT THIS IS: F157 is an owner ruling that the WORD ships bright silver, and it governs the model. A relic print showing the tarnish is showing the measurement, not overruling him — but it must be offered as a separate artefact and named as such, and TARNISH_ZONES (the b-flag, the i-dot, the swash) survives his override anyway, so the b and the i can be shown tarnished with no conflict at all.  ·  ADD A `detail_r` CAMERA. `detail_f` is the nose; there is no tail-side equivalent, and relics 2 and 4 both live there. A few lines in studio.views().  ·  AUTHOR THE HUBCAP SCUFF AND THE ARCH-LIP LOSS FROM ref_side.jpg. Neither exists in the model. They are the two marks the whole set turns on.

**SCREENER.** Six details on black is the default premium-heritage photography move and I have seen it a hundred times; the bus-never-appears constraint is a good discipline but not a fresh idea. The sharp insight is production, not concept — a 274 mm hubcap at 1:1/300 dpi is a normal 3200 px render, so the relic set is CHEAPER than the elevation and is the only thing this chain can honestly print large today. Against that: it is the concept furthest from what the asset can do, since the entire subject is material decay and t1_mats records 'the whole red shell is BLACK -- no edge signal at any arch lip', five materials are still constant roughness, and STATE.md warns the hubcap badge is self-consistency only — so relic 1 would publish an unverified proportion at maximum size.

---

## MISE EN PLACE — The bus removed and its entire galley laid out flat — twenty-eight objects on warm paper, each captioned with its real dimension, like a museum finds tray for the machinery of feeding people.  ·  screen 6.0  ·  `t1-1`

**THE IMAGE.** Pure top-down orthographic flat lay. THE VEHICLE IS GONE. What remains is every object t1_detail.galley_dressing() puts inside it, lifted out and laid on warm off-white paper.

The inventory is real and it is long — STATE.md's own prefix table plus the source: the plancha; `gal_warmer` and `gal_warmer_tap`; four `gal_bot` bottles and their four `gal_botcap` caps; `gal_sqbottle` and `gal_sqcap`; two `gal_caddy` condiment caddies and their two `gal_caddy_fill` contents; three `gal_can_u` and two `gal_can_l`; `gal_wrap` and `gal_wrap_g`, the cling-film roll and its guard; the `gal_rail` and its six `gal_hook`s with their six `gal_tool`s; `gal_rack_hi` (z 1.6840) and `gal_rack_lo` (z 1.5530) with their shelf and two uprights; `gal_pale`; `gal_appliance`; two `gal_stack`; the `gal_tube` strip light and its `gal_tube_ch` channel; and the three pillar menu cards, each a 96 × 311 mm printed card.

Each object is rendered in full colour with one soft contact shadow, and each carries a 0.3 pt gold leader out to a caption in small caps: its name and its actual size in millimetres.

COMPOSITION — and this is the difference between a finds tray and knolling. Not a rigid orthogonal grid. A slightly loose apothecary arrangement, sorted into four functional bands running down the sheet: HEAT (plancha, warmer, tap) / STORE (bottles, cans, caddies, pail) / REACH (rail, hooks, tools, racks, uprights) / TELL (three menu cards, the strip light). Running the full width of the sheet at the foot, under everything, as the only rule on the page: the counter's brass nosing, a single ribbon 2.5 m long in reality. Generous white space — the sheet should feel like a catalogue plate, not a shop window.

Behind the whole array, at 1:1 with the objects and ghosted to about 8%, a 0.3 pt gold outline of the side elevation — so you can locate where each thing came from without the bus competing for attention. It reads last, on the second look, which is exactly right.

LIGHT: single soft top source, flat, no drama, one consistent shadow direction at roughly 15°. Museum-catalogue convention, deliberately. All the warmth comes from the objects' own palette — `GAL_AMBER = (0.4600, 0.2650, 0.0450)`, `GAL_RED = (0.5350, 0.3600, 0.3120)`, `GAL_GREEN = (0.1450, 0.3050, 0.1750)` against `GAL_STEEL = (0.5600, 0.5630, 0.5690)` and the warm `GAL_CREAM = (0.7500, 0.7040, 0.6420)`. That is a seven-colour interior palette this program has never once used, and it is entirely distinct from the exterior's red/cream/gold.

TYPE: the brand's existing deadpan catalogue register, applied to a cling-film roll. Nothing ironic in the setting; the dryness does the work.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** Tenderness for equipment. It is the register of a chef's knife roll photographed on a bench, or a tray of finds laid out by an archaeologist who is quietly delighted — and it says something an exterior shot structurally cannot: feeding people is a set of specific objects, and a person chose every single one of them.

A customer feels affection and a small surprise at the sheer count. A cook feels recognised — that is their rail, their caddies, their roll of film on its spindle. And it is FUNNY, dryly, at about the seven-second mark, when you notice the brand's equipment-catalogue voice being applied with a completely straight face to a plastic pail.

Crucially it is a picture ABOUT LABOUR, in a category whose entire emotional stock is leisure. Every VW-bus poster on the market says *escape*. This one says *someone is at work in there, and this is what they use.*

**FORMATS.** 700 × 1000 mm poster, offset on uncoated — the format the arrangement is designed for  ·  PRINTED TEA TOWEL, 50 × 70 cm. This is the correct physical object for this image and it passes the independent-use test absolutely: it dries dishes. It is also a real merch category the entire VW-bus market ignores, because a bus on a tea towel is meaningless and a kitchen inventory on one is not  ·  Tote panel, 300 mm — the STORE band alone, cropped  ·  12-CARD SET at 90 × 130 mm: one object per card, full bleed, with its dimension and its position in the bus printed on the reverse. Collectible, cheap, and it teaches the poster  ·  Wrapping paper and cup sleeves as a loose repeat of the same objects  ·  Menu inside cover, and a placemat version for the restaurant

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** THERE IS NO VW BUS IN IT. That is the whole defence and it is airtight: the category cannot follow, because a stock VW-bus asset has no galley to lay out. Every competitor is working from an empty shell. Tacombi has twenty-eight modelled objects inside theirs.

It also inverts the merch logic. Instead of putting the vehicle onto an object, it puts the vehicle's objects onto an object — which is why the tea towel is not a gimmick but the argument.

Knolling is the adjacent trope and it is dodged twice: knolling is rigidly orthogonal and cold, this is a loose functional grouping with captions and warmth; and knolling is a photograph of things you own, whereas this is a drawing of things the brand's founding object contains. Nobody has knolled a taqueria.

And it clears the program's blockers wholesale. No emblem in frame, so the owner's ninth-reported defect and F191 are simply not engaged. No body red in frame, so the largest measured colour error in the delivery frame (G/R 0.422 against 0.114) is out of scope. No VW mark anywhere, which is the strongest possible position on the unresolved trademark question. And it is honest about the one ceiling that matters — because each object is shown ALONE, its Y-position is never published, and t1_detail's own docstring says "DEPTH (Y) IS NOT MEASURABLE… Y is therefore chosen, not measured."

**PRODUCTION.** THIS CONCEPT'S REAL COST IS GEOMETRY, NOT LAYOUT, AND IT MUST BE SAID UP FRONT. Several of these objects are boxes and cylinders standing in for things, which is entirely correct at 1.5 m behind a 515 mm aperture and entirely wrong at 200 mm on white paper: `gal_tool` items are boxes, `gal_can_u` and `gal_can_l` are 6 mm slabs, `gal_stack` items are boxes, `gal_caddy_fill` is a filled box. Alone, lit flat, at poster scale, a condiment caddy that is a rounded box will read as a rounded box. Budget REAL FORM for perhaps eight to ten of the twenty-eight. That is genuinely new modelling and it is the honest price of the idea.

THE OFFSET: that work is not thrown away. It improves the galley in every other frame the program will ever make — including SECTION AA and TWENTY-FOUR SECTIONS, both of which show the same objects. Sequence this concept AFTER a decision, but do the geometry FIRST, because all three depend on it.

CAPTIONS GENERATED FROM CONSTANTS, NEVER TYPED. Every millimetre on the sheet must be pulled from source at layout time. The project's own history is a long argument for this.

COLOUR: the GAL_* constants are LINEAR albedos, not sRGB. Convert before specifying inks, and do not sample the render — the render's colour is measurably not the photograph's. Choose and DECLARE the palette with its provenance, then hold it across every artefact.

SHADOW DISCIPLINE. One light, one direction, one softness, for all twenty-eight. Objects rendered in separate passes with drifting shadow angles will look like a collage instantly, and this image lives or dies on looking like one plate.
