Everything this image promises lives in that one addition.  ·  THE EMISSION IS SWAMPED AT STUDIO EXPOSURE, AND THE REGISTER ALREADY MEASURED IT ON A PAINTED WINDOW. t1_mats.py, F134's own comment: "the rendered bead reads sat 0.087 against a photographed 0.561 (ref_side.jpg, painted window), while THIS emission colour's own saturation is 0.558 ... the envelope `base` below has saturation 0.067, which is what the render actually reads, so the null hypothesis is that the emission contributes nothing at studio exposure and we are seeing the base lit by the cyclorama." I CONFIRMED IT BY LOOKING: in out/r75_side.png at 3x the drip-rail chain renders as a row of WHITE PEARLS with no warmth and no spill, while ref_side.jpg at 6x shows an amber line that visibly washes the cream below it. F144 stands. ABLATE `T1_BULB_STR` AND `T1_BULB_BASEV` BEFORE TUNING EITHER (rule 36) — both switches exist, both are measurement-only, and neither changes what ships.  ·  SEVEN SEGMENTS AROUND IS A HEPTAGON AT 27:1. `nu=7, nv=4` is 22 vertices per bulb — invisible at the 5-7 px a bulb occupies in the side render, unmissable at macro. Raise `nu`/`nv` for a macro build behind its own switch. (Note this is exactly the property CONCEPT 3 keeps on purpose; the two concepts want opposite builds and both are legitimate.)  ·  THE FLEX IS A SQUARE WIRE. `T.sweep(wire, [(0.0026, 0.0026), (0.0026, -0.0026), (-0.0026, -0.0026), (-0.0026, 0.0026)], ...)` is a 5.2 x 5.2 mm SQUARE section. Flex is round. And in ref_side.jpg the tail-board run hangs as a SLACK CATENARY drooping well below its anchors, while `bulbflex` follows the rail rigidly. The sag is the drawing in this image — build it.  ·  PUSH THE EMITTER ABOVE THE BLOOM THRESHOLD SO IT ACTUALLY FLARES. `T1_BULB_STR` ships at 9.0 and post.py's `BLOOM_THR` is 0.94. Drop the key, raise the emitter, and check the flare is coming from the compositor rather than from clipping — a clipped white core is not a flare and will print as a flat disc.  ·  EXPLOIT THE IRIS THAT ALREADY EXISTS AND HAS NEVER BEEN USED. `aim()` sets `aperture_blades = 9` and `aperture_rotation = math.radians(11)`, so out-of-focus speculars come back as 9-SIDED amber polygons rather than perfect discs. That single detail is the difference between this reading as a photograph and reading as a Gaussian blur, and it is already in the code.

**SCREENER.** Emotionally direct and beautifully specified, and the 9-sided iris already sitting unused in aim() is lovely craft. But shallow-DOF fairy-light bokeh is close to the most photographed image on earth — this is a stock photo with better provenance, and craft is not originality. Worst buildability in the set by some distance: it is named after a filament the model does not have (the bulb is one opaque 7x4 ball), and F144 already grades the model-side lever EXHAUSTED on the exact element the whole concept rests on.

---

## MACHINED — THE RISKY ONE — abandon photorealism at exactly the scale where it breaks: four hard-shaded, visibly faceted macro posters that show the object as it is actually built, twenty-eight flat quadrilaterals and all.  ·  screen 5.0  ·  `t7-2`

**THE IMAGE.** FOUR POSTERS, 500 x 700 mm portrait. ONE hard key at 35 degrees upper-left, one weak fill, black shadow, NO bounce, NO environment, NO bloom, NO grain, NO vignette, NO denoiser mush. FLAT SHADING throughout — every facet is a single flat tone with a hard terminator at every edge. Nothing is smoothed and nothing pretends.

POSTER 1 — THE BULB, ~27:1, filling two-thirds of the sheet. A SEVEN-SIDED BARREL with four latitude rings: twenty-eight quadrilateral faces, each one a distinct flat value stepping from near-white on the key side, through four or five warm mid-greys, to a single near-black facet on the shadow side. It reads as a cut gem, a brass nut, a twelve-sided die — anything but a light. The square-section flex enters bottom-left as a hard 5.2 mm cable. KEPT, NOT FIXED: at this scale the square section is honest and it is beautiful.
POSTER 2 — THE DOME, 2.2:1. The hubcap as a faceted hemisphere, the 96-segment cap ring stepping visibly around it, and the badge rendered as a FLAT CRISP GRAPHIC with no relief shadow at all — the black halo that ruins it photoreally simply does not exist under flat shading.
POSTER 3 — THE TIP, 23:1. The tail board's end, where `seg=4` corners read as FOUR VISIBLE CHAMFERS rather than a radius, and the three colour bands — near-black, cream, red — meet each chamfer as hard mitres. Six flat shapes and eight edges.
POSTER 4 — THE CORNER, 4:1. The counter's 150 mm plan radius as a POLYGON, the brass nosing stepping round it in visible flats, the tan slab above and cream fascia below meeting it in two straight lines.

PALETTE: three or four FLAT INKS, DECLARED not sampled — one red, one cream, one brass, one near-black. No gradient anywhere on any sheet except the facet steps themselves, and those are STEPS, not gradients. Riso or screen print, so the ink sits ON the paper and the flats are physically flat.

TYPE ON THE FACE: NONE. One blind-embossed chop bottom-right. The dimension and the part name go on the REVERSE, letterpressed, where only the owner sees them.

**EMOTIONAL CLAIM.** WANTING TO PICK IT UP. Facets make an object feel HELD — cut, turned, machined, finished by a hand on a lathe. At 27:1 a bulb with seven visible flats stops being a light and becomes a jewel, a nut, a die: a well-made small thing. That is a tactile pleasure photorealism can never deliver, because photorealism asks you to BELIEVE and faceting invites you to TOUCH. And there is a second, quieter feeling underneath it: the sheets are honest. This is what we actually built. It rhymes exactly with the vehicle's own origin — a $3,000 bus, missing three windows, cut open with a grinder — because it is a picture of construction rather than a picture of a finish.

**FORMATS.** POSTER SET OF FOUR, 500 x 700 mm, RISO or screen print on uncoated 250 gsm. Riso IS this aesthetic: three spot inks, no halftone, hard edges, visible mis-registration of half a millimetre that makes the flats sit on the paper rather than in it. Do not offset this; offset would smooth exactly what the concept is about.  ·  ENAMEL PIN of the faceted bulb, 25 mm. Seven facets is a LOW enough count to survive hard enamel at 25 mm — a photoreal bulb at that size is a blob, a seven-sided one is a legible object. This is the format the concept was born for.  ·  EMBROIDERED PATCH, 60 mm, merrowed edge. Satin stitch wants flat panels bounded by hard edges, which is precisely what flat shading produces. Four to five thread colours, one per value step.  ·  CERAMIC COASTER / TILE SET, 100 mm square, four designs. Flat colour and hard edges are what ceramic decals do best; a gradient would band.  ·  MOTION: a slow 6 fps STEPPED orbit around a single faceted bulb, 48 steps, no motion blur, no easing. The facets flip value one at a time as the key crosses each edge — the whole animation is twenty-eight surfaces taking turns. 1080 x 1080, silent, seamless.  ·  NOT a large-format photoreal-substitute hero. This is a graphic system, not a picture of a vehicle, and scaling it to a two-metre wall piece would just be big flat shapes.

**WHY NOT DERIVATIVE.** THIS CONTRADICTS THE ENTIRE PREMISE OF THE PROJECT AND I AM PROPOSING IT ANYWAY. Seventy-five revisions were spent chasing photorealism; this throws it away at the one scale where photorealism is losing, and says so out loud. A cautious team rejects it in the first meeting — 'it looks unfinished', 'it looks like a 1993 render', 'we paid for a photoreal model'. All three are true and none is an objection. The VW-bus category has exactly two poles, photoreal and flat-vector; a hard-shaded faceted CAD object is neither, reads as machined prototype or early-CGI or Memphis-era product render, and there is nothing remotely like it on that shelf. It is also structurally impossible to mistake for a photograph of a VW bus, because there is no bus in frame and no photography in the treatment. AND IT IS THE ONLY ONE OF THE THREE THAT IS IMMUNE TO EVERY OPEN DEFECT AT ONCE: the salmon red becomes a chosen ink; the gloss deficit is irrelevant because there is no specular; the emblem's arrangement becomes a design decision instead of a nine-times-rejected fidelity failure; the mottle and the denoiser boil vanish under flat shading.

**PRODUCTION.** THE FACETING IS ALREADY IN THE MESH — THIS COSTS ALMOST NOTHING AND THAT IS THE POINT. `_ball(..., nu=7, nv=4)` gives the bulb twenty-eight quads. `T.rrect(TB_CHORD, TB_WIDTH, 0.012, seg=4)` gives the tail board four-segment corners. The cap ring is `seg=96`. The tail-board edge prisms are `seg=3` and `seg=4`. The tyre is `TREAD_SEG * TREAD_LUGS` = 384. Every one of those numbers is a design asset the moment you stop hiding it.

TURN OFF `shade_smooth` AND TURN OFF THE OPTICS. `T1_FX=0`, and pass post.py `--backdrop white` explicitly — its live constant is `BACKDROP = "headroom"` at peak 252, which would put a grey field inside a white margin. Bloom, CA, vignette and grain all actively fight a hard-edge flat image.

DO NOT USE `clay_all()` OR ANY MATERIAL OVERRIDE. It clears every material slot and throws away the artwork that IS this vehicle's identity, leaving a grey van. The faceting must come from NORMALS, not from materials: flat-shade and keep the diffuse colour.

PALETTE MUST BE DECLARED, NOT EYEDROPPERED. I looked at the render's rear hubcap at 6x: the dome is salmon-pink with a hard plastic crescent highlight, where ref_side.jpg's is oxblood with a soft sweep. This concept does not need the render's red to be right — it chooses its ink — but nobody may sample it off a frame. Reconcile the three authored reds into one and write down which: t1_mats `RED = (0.5520, 0.0294, 0.0176)` = sRGB(196,49,36); lid_gen `RED = (159, 45, 22)`; cal_gen `RED = (214, 46, 30)`.

Riso spot inks: specify by ink name, not by CMYK build. Fluorescent orange under the brass gives the nosing a glow no process build can reach.

**MODEL WORK REQUIRED.** THE SAME MACRO CAMERA CLASS AS 'AS MEASURED' — 100-200 mm lens at 0.15-0.5 m — but at f/22 with everything sharp. Nothing here is croppable from existing frames: at the side render's ~272 px/m a 22.0 mm bulb is 6 px. `studio.views()` has no macro entry; `detail_f` stands at ~3.5 m.  ·  FLAT SHADING SWITCH. The shell is `shade_smooth` and the whole concept depends on turning that off without touching topology. One switch, applied at the object level, with a watched kill — render the bulb smooth and flat and put the crops side by side before believing it.  ·  KEEP THE LOW SEGMENT COUNTS. This is the one item that is a DECISION rather than a task, and it is the opposite of what CONCEPT 2 needs: `nu=7, nv=4` on the bulb and `seg=4` on the tail-board corners must be PRESERVED for the macro build. If a future revision raises them for photoreal reasons, this concept quietly dies. Record it, or add a `T1_MACRO_FACET` build that pins them.  ·  NO PASS INFRASTRUCTURE EXISTS, AND THE ENAMEL / RISO / EMBROIDERY TIERS NEED IT. `grep pass_index|use_pass` returns nothing outside studio.py's denoiser config — no ID pass, no normal pass, no AO pass, no cryptomatte. For the POSTER tier this is not required (Blender can render the flats directly). For anything that needs per-facet flat regions as vector — the pin, the patch, the Riso separations — a material/object ID pass is the shortest route and is a contained build.  ·  NOTHING ELSE. NO FIDELITY WORK AT ALL. This concept requires no emblem fix, no red correction, no gloss work, no tread measurement, no bulb filament, and no resolution proving run beyond a 500 x 700 mm sheet at 300 dpi (5906 x 8268 px — above the proven 3840, so ONE stitch run must be exercised and its exit code read). It is the only one of the three that could ship while every open finding in the register stays open, and it is the reason to consider it despite the risk.

**SCREENER.** The sharpest single observation in the brief — the faceting is ALREADY in the mesh, so the aesthetic costs nothing — and the enamel pin is exactly the right format for a 7-sided bulb. Fails the test that matters most here: nothing about it is about THIS bus or THIS brand. Hard-shaded low-poly-as-retro-CGI is a whole exhausted design trend, and the same four posters could be made from any object at all. Its self-declared bravery (throw away the photorealism) is also its weakness — being contrarian about the pipeline is not the same as having an idea about the vehicle.
